#10229. How Motion Design Improves SaaS Onboarding and Feature Adoption

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Most SaaS products don't lose users because the software is bad. They lose users in the first few minutes, before anyone has seen what the product can actually do. Someone signs up, lands on an empty dashboard, feels a flicker of confusion, and quietly leaves. The industry has a name for the gap between signing up and getting value: activation. Closing it is one of the hardest problems in product design.

Motion design is one of the most underused tools for closing that gap. Used well, animation isn't decoration sprinkled on at the end. It's a layer of communication that tells users where to look, confirms that their actions worked, and makes a complex interface feel learnable instead of overwhelming. This article looks at why motion influences behaviour during onboarding, the specific patterns that move the needle on activation and feature adoption, and how to build them responsibly as a developer — with an eye on performance and accessibility throughout.

Motion works in onboarding because it speaks to how human perception actually operates, not because it looks modern. A few principles do most of the heavy lifting.

It directs attention. The eye is drawn to movement before it reads a single word. A gently pulsing button or an element that slides into view tells a new user where to go next without a paragraph of instructions. On a crowded first-run screen, that guidance is the difference between a confident first action and a confused exit.

It preserves context. When a panel expands, a card flips, or a new view slides in from the right, a short transition shows users where things came from and where they went. This spatial continuity keeps people oriented. Interfaces that snap instantly between states force the user to re-scan the screen each time and silently rebuild their mental model.

It confirms actions. Every click, toggle, and form submission is a small question: did that work? A quick visual response — a checkmark drawing itself, a row settling into place — answers it immediately. Without that feedback, users repeat actions, doubt the system, or assume something broke.

It shapes perceived performance. A thoughtful loading animation or skeleton screen makes a wait feel shorter and the product feel responsive, even when the underlying request takes exactly as long. Perception of speed is part of the experience, and motion is how you manage it.

These are the patterns that reliably help new users reach their first win.

Onboarding doesn't end after the first session. Most SaaS products keep shipping features, and each one faces its own adoption problem: users settle into a few familiar workflows and never discover the rest. Motion is how you reintroduce the product to people who already think they know it.

Contextual nudges that appear in motion — a tooltip that eases in near a newly relevant button, a gentle highlight on a feature the moment a user hits the limit it solves — are far more effective than a generic email. They arrive at the point of need and use movement to pull attention without hijacking the screen.

"What's new" reveals can announce an update with a small, well-timed animation rather than a modal that gets dismissed on reflex. A badge that animates in, or a panel that slides open on the first visit to a page, respects the user's flow while still being noticed.

Animated demonstrations carry the most weight for genuinely complex features. Showing a feature in motion — rather than describing it in text — lets users grasp a multi-step workflow in seconds. For intricate capabilities, this is where a short, purpose-made explainer earns its place, a point we return to in the next section.

The right tool depends on how complex the animation is and where it lives. Roughly in order of weight:

CSS transitions and keyframe animations should be your default. They cover the large majority of onboarding motion — hovers, state changes, highlights, progress bars — and the browser can run them efficiently. Reach for JavaScript only when CSS genuinely can't express what you need.

The Web Animations API gives you CSS-level performance with programmatic control, which matters when animations depend on data or user input, or need to be sequenced and interrupted. SitePoint's guide to the Web Animations API is a solid introduction, and the broader web animation archive covers libraries like GSAP for orchestrating complex timelines.

Lottie is the bridge between designer-made animation and the web. Designers export vector animations as JSON, and a lightweight player renders them natively at small file sizes — ideal for polished illustrative moments like success states and animated empty states, without hand-coding every frame.

Pre-rendered video is the right call when a feature is genuinely involved and a looping CSS animation or a tooltip simply can't carry the explanation. A short, well-produced walkthrough can compress a multi-step workflow into something a user absorbs in under a minute, which is why many teams lean on a dedicated SaaS explainer video for their most complex features and onboarding moments. The trade-off is weight and production effort, so reserve video for the handful of places where it genuinely outperforms in-interface motion, lazy-load it, and never autoplay it with sound.

Motion that stutters does more damage than no motion at all, and motion that ignores accessibility can actively harm users. Two disciplines keep animation on the right side of that line.

For performance, animate the properties the browser can handle cheaply: transform and opacity. These can run on the compositor thread without triggering layout or paint, which is what lets animation hold a smooth 60fps. Animating properties like width, height, top, or left forces the browser to recalculate layout on every frame and is the usual cause of janky motion. Keep animations short — most interface transitions should land between 150 and 300 milliseconds — because motion that overstays its welcome makes a product feel slow.

For accessibility, honour the prefers-reduced-motion media query. Some users experience dizziness, nausea, or disorientation from movement, and the operating system lets them ask for less of it. Respecting that setting is straightforward:

Beyond that, never let an animation block interaction. If a user has to wait for a flourish to finish before they can click, the motion is working against them. Animation should support the task and then get out of the way.

Motion design is a product decision, so treat it like one and measure it. The metrics that matter map directly to the problems motion is meant to solve.

Treat animations as hypotheses and A/B test them. Ship the animated onboarding step to half of your new users and measure activation against the control. Motion should earn its place with data, not assumptions — and occasionally the data will tell you an animation is slowing people down, which is just as useful to know.

The best onboarding motion is the kind users never consciously notice. It simply makes the product feel clear, responsive, and easy to learn — guiding attention, confirming actions, and turning a complex interface into something approachable. That's the real payoff: not animation for its own sake, but fewer confused users, faster time to value, and features that actually get discovered.

Start small. Pick the single point in your onboarding where users most often stall, add one purposeful animation that removes the confusion, and measure what happens. Motion, used deliberately, is one of the most direct levers you have on activation and adoption — and like any good tool, it works best when it draws no attention to itself.

#10224. Integrating TorchTPU into TorchTitan and Optimizing Models for TPU

Over the past years, Meta’s TorchTitan has emerged as the PyTorch ecosystem’s premier clean-room reference platform for large-scale generative AI model training. Built entirely on PyTorch-native scaling primitives, TorchTitan provides an unencumbered, highly modular showcase for pre-training flagship dense and Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures like Llama 3, Qwen 3, and FLUX.

Historically, developers looking to train PyTorch models on Google Cloud TPUs faced some challenges: either undertake a complete codebase rewrite into JAX, or grapple with legacy torch_xla. That paradigm has officially shifted. With the launch of TorchTPU, Cloud TPUs now feature a native backend (torch.device("tpu")) integrated directly into PyTorch's core interface. Enabling a training script to use TPUs now only requires minimal changes to your device initialization code. Providing device compatibility, however, is only half the battle, developers also need high performance production-grade, distributed training recipes. This is where TorchTitan on TPU comes in. TorchTitan TPU is a fork of Meta’s TorchTitan implementation that is adapted to enable model training on Google Cloud TPUs. The fork is available at https://github.com/google-pytorch/torchtitan/

Rather than requiring engineering teams to invent custom TPU sharding strategies or hand-craft low-level XLA communication passes from scratch, Google and Meta co-developed TPU-native execution passes directly inside TorchTitan. TorchTitan serves as the definitive vehicle for scaling PyTorch on Google Cloud TPUs.

TorchTitan provides official, battle-tested recipes for pre-training large dense models (such as Llama 3 70B/405B and Qwen 3 8B/32B), Mixture-of-Experts (such as Qwen 3 235B MoE), and diffusion transformers (such as FLUX.1 12B).

Beyond initial pre-training, TorchTitan serves as the scalable trainer inside post-training pipelines like VeRL for Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) and Reinforcement Learning (RL) alignment. In these workflows, TorchTitan handles the parameter update step while synchronizing weights with high-throughput serving engines like vLLM-TorchTPU.

Parallelism is also kept separate from the model code: techniques such as fully sharded data parallelism and tensor parallelism are layered onto a parallelism-agnostic model implementation. The exception is expert parallelism: the MoE layer performs explicit communication through a pluggable token dispatcher that each backend can implement in its own way.

TorchTitan builds a model from a configuration tree, and that tree can be rewritten before the model is constructed. This offers a clean way to replace individual blocks of a model with versions that run better on TPU, without touching the model definition or the shared trainer loop.

We use this actively. The TPU recipes replace standard attention with Splash Attention, a Pallas kernel that handles causal, sliding-window and packed-document masks without materializing the attention matrix; replace the final projection and cross-entropy loss with a fused Pallas kernel that never writes the full logits tensor to HBM; and replace MoE expert matmuls with grouped Pallas kernels. Each kernel is a JAX function turned into a PyTorch custom op by TorchTPU's torch.tpu.pallas.jax_op decorator, which is all the glue a new kernel needs. Each replacement is a config flag, so a recipe can turn it on, and a test can compare it against the plain PyTorch path it replaces. Kernels can come from tokamax, the OpenXLA kernel library for TPU and GPU.

FSDP2 is built around the GPU execution model. Its all-gathers and reduce-scatters are issued from module hooks as explicit collective calls, and overlap with compute comes from running them on dedicated CUDA streams synchronized with events. None of that is visible to a compiler: the collectives sit outside the traced region, so on TPU a torch.compile region breaks at every FSDP hook and a whole-step graph cannot be captured at all. TorchTPU provides no-op stream and event objects only so that FSDP2 runs; XLA has no use for them, since it schedules work from data dependencies.

SimpleFSDP takes the compiler's view instead. Parameter sharding is a DTensor parametrization: reading a module's weight performs a redistribute from sharded to replicated, which is the all-gather, and its autograd backward is the reduce-scatter. There are no hooks and no explicit collective calls, so both collectives are ordinary nodes in the traced graph, whether that graph is a per-block torch.compile region or the Graph Trainer's single make_fx step. The XLA compiler schedules the all-gathers ahead of the matmuls that need them and overlaps the reduce-scatters with the backward compute, inside one program. Mixed precision is part of the same mechanism, a policy that sets the gather dtype (bf16 parameters) and the reduce dtype (fp32 or bf16 gradients) on that redistribute. Hybrid sharding for multislice is the same code on a 2-D mesh: shard within a slice over ICI, replicate across slices over DCN.

On FLUX this is what brought TorchTitan to 99% performance parity with JAX MaxDiffusion: the per-layer HLO for a SimpleFSDP block is a single module with the weight all-gathers, matmuls, attention and norms fused together, which is the shape of program XLA was built to optimize.

Many PyTorch trainers either run in eager mode with CUDA graphs to cut launch overhead, or apply torch.compile to isolated regions, typically one transformer block at a time. TorchTPU does not yet support the PyTorch torch.Stream abstraction, which limits the performance of mixing eager-based FSDP2 with layer-wise torch.compile. SimpleFSDP helps by expressing parameter sharding in a way that is compatible with compilation, whether using layer-wise torch.compile or full-graph capture.

TorchTitan's Graph Trainer provides full training-step capture (forward, loss and backward), producing one FX graph ahead of time with PyTorch's make_fx tracer, skipping AOTAutograd partitioning, so the whole computation is visible to XLA). GraphTrainer also runs optimization passes: to implement graph-based activation memory policy that decides what to save, recompute or offload. The train step then executes as a single XLA program.

This is the model XLA was built for, and it makes PyTorch training on TPU conceptually the same as JAX, where the whole train step is one jitted function. Owning the whole step as one graph is also what makes the large-model compilation techniques possible: scan over layers, which runs the decoder layers as a single loop body so that compile time and memory stop growing with depth, and the graph splitter, which cuts the step into separately compiled forward and backward programs with activations offloaded to host memory in between. On Qwen3 235B this path reaches 29%+ MFU.

The optimizer can join the graph as well: While the fused_adam optimizer is very performant, a functional AdamW update can be traced into the train-step (functional_adamw_step) and ensures an iteration has no host-side work between the first forward op and the weight write-back. On Qwen3-8B this path reaches 51-52% MFU.

Every lever below is described in the TorchTitan TPU optimization guide, and nearly all of them are already wired into the recipes. For most models, getting a good MFU on TPU is a sequence of configuration changes on an existing recipe rather than new code. The order matters, because each step changes what the next one measures. A lot of optimizations in TorchTitan are available as ready-to-use building blocks that can be enabled by a flag flip (see illustration below).

4. Tune Compiler, Communication and Profile

To check that TorchTitan runs efficiently across different classes of models, we tuned and scaled several of them on TPU v7x (Ironwood). At scale, Llama 3 405B (large dense model) holds about 57% MFU from 128 to 1,024 chips. Qwen3 235B (large MoE model) reaches 32.42% MFU on a single 1,024-chip slice and 30.51% on 8,192 chips across eight slices. Muse Glimmer 30B (modern dense model) reaches 43.4% MFU on 128 chips (4x4x8). FLUX.1 (diffusion model) reaches 19.4% MFU on 1,024 chips (8x8x16).

The following case studies show how we got there for three of these models, each representing a different class:

FLUX.1 is a 12-billion-parameter diffusion transformer, which makes it a useful check that the playbook above is not specific to LLMs. We trained it at 1024x1024 resolution on a TPU v6e-8 with FSDP in bf16 and a batch of three samples. Out of the box, in eager mode with FSDP2 and full activation checkpointing, a step took 11.49 seconds. Applying the playbook in order brought that down one stage at a time. Deferred-and-fused eager execution cut the number of HLO module invocations by 28% and the step to 9.95 s (15.5% improvement). Replacing FSDP2 with SimpleFSDP reduced the step to 6.52 s (52.6% improvement): the trace was dominated by FSDP2 copying parameter shards into a flat staging buffer before each all-gather and back out into per-parameter tensors afterwards; SimpleFSDP all-gathers each parameter directly, so the staging buffer and both copies are gone (see the SimpleFSDP section above). The splash attention kernel, with sequence packing and segment ids so that no MXU cycles go to padding, took the step to 5.55 s (17.5% improvement), and per-block torch.compile, which hands XLA large sub-graphs instead of fine-grained modules and moves control flow out of the Python interpreter, took it to 3.02 s (83.8% improvement).

The final step came from three small, local rewrites in the model code that the profile pointed at: splitting a fused projection into two separate linear layers so its output no longer had to be sliced apart, simplifying the rotary embedding to plain element-wise math, and unpacking Q, K and V with a view instead of a transpose. None of them changes what the model computes; each removes copies and lets XLA fuse more of the graph. Together they brought the step to 2.78 s (8.6% improvement), 4.1x faster than the starting point and about 99% of the throughput of the JAX-based MaxDiffusion on the same hardware.

We also scaled FLUX from 8 to 1,024 v7x chips, keeping three images per TPU. The best configuration went from 2.53 s per step (20.76% MFU) on 8 chips to 2.71 s (19.4% MFU) on 1,024, which is 93% weak-scaling efficiency. Past one 64-chip cube, pure FSDP slows down due to the model’s compact size. Hybrid sharding via HSDP fixes this: it shards within each 4x4x4 cube and replicates across cubes. We used the topology-aware device mesh, which maps the parallelism optimally to the physical 8x8x16 torus.

Muse Glimmer 30B is a modern dense decoder: 52 layers, grouped-query attention with 32 query and 2 key-value heads, a 202k-token vocabulary with a soft-capped output head, and a layer pattern that mixes sliding-window and global attention, with RoPE skipped on every fourth layer. It is the case that shows how much works out of the box. The model definition, the parallelization and the training loop ran on TPU unchanged; the port came down to two model-specific pieces. Upstream implements the sliding-window pattern with FlexAttention, which has no TPU backend, so the first step was a windowed SDPA inner attention that reproduces the window exactly and gives a correctness baseline, checked against upstream on CPU. The second piece was the fused cross-entropy kernel, which we extended to apply the soft-cap to each vocabulary tile as it computes the loss, so the 202k-wide logits never have to be materialized; without that the LM head alone dominated memory. Everything else, including the Graph Trainer wrapper, was the standard onboarding ladder, with each feature behind a flag and its own test. The resulting recipe trains the 30B on 64 v7x chips (a 4x4x4 slice) at sequence length 16,384 with one sequence per device, Graph Trainer plus SimpleFSDP and fp32 gradient reductions, and reaches 5,848 tokens per second per chip at ~44% MFU.

Qwen3-235B-A22B is the largest MOE model in this set: 94 layers, 128 experts with 8 active per token, 235 billion parameters of which 22 billion are active per token. It is the case that exercises the large-model half of the playbook. At this depth compiling the whole unrolled step as one program is not an option: XLA plans every buffer of a program at compile time, and a 94-layer graph laid out flat asks for more HBM than the chip has before a step can run, while its compile time grows with every layer. Getting it to work at all meant enabling one of the two Graph Trainer paths for large models. Scan over layers is the XLA-native answer, the same construct JAX and MaxText rely on: the 94 identical layers become one compiled loop body, so compile time and program size stop growing with depth. The graph splitter is the more flexible alternative: it keeps the layers unrolled but cuts the captured step into per-layer executables, which works no matter what kind of layers you have (and does not require layers to be the same). We run both for this model and they land within a few percent of each other. Either way the activations do not fit in HBM at sequence length 4096 with 16 sequences per device, so both paths offload layer activations to host memory and prefetch them back for the backward pass.

From there the playbook applied, one change at a time, each measured on the same 64-chip slice. We raised the scoped VMEM budget so the compiler could keep more kernel intermediates on chip, which also removed an exposed update at the tail of the optimizer step. We enabled a set of libtpu scheduling flags that let XLA's latency-hiding scheduler overlap the FSDP all-gathers and reduce-scatters with the surrounding compute and offload the reduce-scatters to the SparseCores, so most of the collective time is hidden behind compute, leaving only a small exposed reduce-scatter tail in the backward pass. We switched attention to the tokamax splash kernel, and replaced the expert matmuls with specialized paired grouped-matmul kernels backed by a K-tiled weight-gradient kernel, with hierarchical reduce-scatter and the SparseCore latency-hiding scheduler to hide the expert-gradient collectives. A fused route-score backward removed a large fp32 copy from the MoE backward pass. The last round of fixes came from profiling at 1,024 chips: keeping the blocked activation layout under scan offload, raising the backward layer-scheduler threshold so the expert reduce-scatters overlap with compute again, and pipelining small reduce-scatters across the scan loop's back-edge. The recipe runs at ~32% MFU on 256 v7x chips with fp32 gradient reductions kept throughout.

We also used this model to test how far TorchTPU scales, from 64 chips up to 8,192-chip v7x pod scale, in both single-slice and multi-slice configurations. A TPU slice is a set of chips connected directly over the inter-chip interconnect (ICI); multislice lets one training job span several slices connected over the data-center network (DCN), which is how a job grows past the largest single slice. Within a slice we run FSDP over ICI, sharding parameters, gradients and optimizer state across the slice's chips; across slices the model is replicated and the gradients are all-reduced over DCN once per step, so the only traffic on the slower network is a single gradient reduction, which overlaps with the backward pass. That is hybrid sharded data parallelism (HSDP), and it is what the 2,048- to 8,192-chip runs use, with 1,024-chip slices as the sharding group. The framework behaved as expected throughout: per-chip throughput dropped by 8% going from 64 to 8,192 chips (4,568 to 4,208 tokens per second per chip), near-linear scaling across 128x more chips. One caveat: for a model of this size, data parallelism beyond about 1K chips is a scaling experiment rather than a training recipe. This is a weak-scaling run: the batch per device stays fixed as chips are added, so the global batch grows with the chip count and at 8k chips is larger than one would use to train this model. What the run shows is that the framework, the compiler and the collectives hold up at that scale, not that this is the configuration to train with.

The three case studies above are deep dives: one model at a time, with a parity test and a profile behind every change. A team arriving on TPU usually has a different problem. They have a model they already train, often a Hugging Face one, and quite likely it won’t be the model that TorchTitan ships for TPU. They may well want every last percent out of it eventually, but first they need an easy way to get it running at all. That is the problem the Transformers modeling backend solves: it builds the model from the Transformers library by its Hub id and runs it through TorchTitan's trainer and parallelism instead of its own. On TPU the backend has a TorchTPU lane with a single configuration that applies the first rungs of the playbook optimizations to any supported checkpoint, with no change to the model code: SimpleFSDP in place of FSDP2, per-block torch.compile, the splash attention kernel registered as an attention implementation with the transformers attention interface so the model's own attention calls land on it, the fused Pallas cross entropy fed the final hidden states and the head weight instead of materialized logits, and, for mixture-of-experts checkpoints, the TPU grouped-matmul experts substituted for the portable implementation. Most of the dense architectures the backend supports train this way unmodified; mixture-of-experts checkpoints run through the same path but without expert parallelism yet. None of this reaches the hand-tuned numbers above, but it is a working, reasonably efficient first run for a model that has no TPU recipe yet, and a way to find out whether it deserves one.

Sooner or later a model deserves more than a generic path: it is the one you will train for months, it has an architecture the backend cannot express, or the out-of-the-box number is not the one you need. Porting a model to a new accelerator used to be a specialist project measured in weeks, with the hard-won lessons living in one engineer's head. On TorchTitan TPU it is a repeatable procedure. The repository ships a model-onboarding skill for coding agents that captures how the case studies above were done and walks an engineer, or the agent itself, through the same ladder, with the tests, the acceptance bars and the traps built in.

The roadmap runs along five lines: production readiness, precision, hardware, parallelism and models. On production readiness, the recipes gain the operational tooling that long runs on Google Cloud expect: integration with the ML Goodput Measurement library, so a run reports how much of its chip time turns into preserved training progress and where the rest goes, and with Multi-Tier Checkpointing on GKE, which keeps checkpoints in local memory and in-cluster replicas with Cloud Storage as the durable tier, so large jobs save often and resume quickly. On precision, the float8 recipe that today ships for Llama 3 70B, built on torchao's Float8Linear and v7x's native fp8 matmuls, extends to the rest of the model set. On hardware, Google has announced its eighth-generation TPUs, TPU 8t for pre-training and TPU 8i for inference and reinforcement learning, and TorchTitan will support them as they become available on Google Cloud, with the same recipes carried forward. On parallelism, expert parallelism for the MoE kernels is the open item named earlier, and long-context training is next after it, now that million-token context windows are the norm for frontier models. On models, the targets are the next size up from Qwen3-235B: Qwen3.8-Max, a 2.4-trillion-parameter mixture of experts, and Kimi K3, 2.8 trillion parameters with Kimi Delta Attention in place of standard attention on most layers, which will exercise the kernel plug-in path as much as the scaling one.

TorchTitan on TPU is no longer an experiment. The same codebase and plain-PyTorch model definitions train Llama 3 405B at about 57% MFU on up to 1,024 chips, and run Qwen3 235B, a mixture-of-experts model, on up to 1,024 chips in a single slice. In a weak-scaling test with a fixed per-chip batch, Qwen3 235B kept 92% of its per-chip throughput from 64 to 8,192 chips. That shows the framework, compiler and collectives hold up at pod scale, even though a model of this size would not be trained at that scale. The pieces that make this possible are not buried in one recipe. SimpleFSDP, the Graph Trainer with scan and the graph splitter, host offload, the Pallas kernel library and the optimization playbook are building blocks, each behind a flag and each documented with the number it is worth. For models that TorchTitan does not ship, the Transformers modeling backend runs 500+ Hugging Face models through the same trainer with the first optimizations already applied, and for teams that want more than that, the onboarding skill turns porting a model into a repeatable procedure that an engineer or an agent can follow.

That leaves two ways to use it. The first is to adopt TorchTitan as the training framework: pick a recipe, launch it on a slice, and build on a configuration whose performance is already known. The second is to keep the training code you have and take what you need: the kernels are standard PyTorch custom ops, SimpleFSDP and the Graph Trainer are modules you can import, the compiler flags and the playbook apply to any TorchTPU workload, and every recipe spells out what it enables and why. Either way, the fastest way to find out what this does for your model is to run it.

#10222. Meet the 20 first-place apps from RevenueCat’s Shipaton 2026

A cozy habit tracker, a smarter snippet library, and a rooster that wants you out of bed: this year’s Shipaton winners offer plenty of new apps to explore.

RevenueCat’s annual app-building competition drew 3,985 project submissions in 2026, according to its winner announcement. Across the 20 first-place categories, developers tackled everyday frustrations, built inventive games, and found new ways to connect people.

Here’s a look at every first-place winner:

Read RevenueCat’s full winner announcement

Keeping a routine is easier when there’s something to look forward to. Mio pairs self-care and habit tracking with a pixel companion that grows as you make progress. Check off habits, earn coins, and unlock new companions and collectibles, with friends and groups adding a social reason to keep going.

Mio took Shipaton’s Grand Prize, with RevenueCat highlighting its revenue and growth in a crowded habit-tracking market.

That dog you met on your morning walk can become part of your collection. Boop identifies cats and dogs and turns their photos into stickers for a digital book, giving pet lovers a playful reason to pay attention to the animals around them.

Creator Taiwo Omisore’s “100 Boops” challenge took the app into the real world, where printed pet portraits and conversations with owners helped shape the product. That hands-on approach earned Boop first place in #BuildInPublic.

If you have fond memories of futuristic arcade racers, Nitelore has a familiar appeal: hovering vehicles, neon tracks, and plenty of speed. Chase your own ghost in time trials, take on combat races, or compete online.

Free tracks offer a starting point, while paid unlocks and daily and weekly challenges give players more to explore. RevenueCat’s Best Game judges singled out the art direction, effects, and haptics.

Explaining how you see color can be surprisingly difficult. See as Me: Colorblind makes that conversation visual, using a split-screen camera to compare different types and degrees of color blindness. A shareable link lets someone else see the comparison without installing the app.

Developer Javier Jiménez drew on his own experience with protanopia. That personal perspective helped the app earn the RevenueCat Peace Prize.

Clucky’s rooster is determined to get you out of bed. Turning off an alarm can require solving a problem, moving around, or finding an object, bringing a little friction to the temptation to snooze.

Its personality runs through the entire interface, including a time picker that uses the rooster’s scrolling eyes and yellow beak. That consistent character, animation, and attention to detail earned Clucky the RevenueCat Design Award.

Adversary starts with a familiar challenge: flip cards and find matching pairs. The catch is a hidden Danger card with no matching partner. Reveal it, and an ad becomes part of the board you’re trying to remember.

That idea earned Niv Ben-porath’s game the Catvertising Award. Players can continue with ads after an initial ad-free period, or choose a permanent unlock that removes ads and adds difficulty options.

Put a few phones on the table, and Bubble Games turns their screens into a shared game board. Objects move between devices as friends compete in minigames, including a virtual hot potato.

Built by four software engineering students at Université Laval, the Next Gen Award winner uses the phones people already have to create a shared activity. Its Shipaton submission includes ten minigames. The winner announcement links to the Android release on Google Play.

GRIDLOCK takes a game everyone knows and adds up to four players, online rooms, and eleven special moves. The result offers more ways to disrupt an opponent’s plans than the familiar three-by-three board.

Its HAMM Award win recognizes how the business model fits the game: a shared Credits currency connects rewarded ads, optional packs, and a subscription, while core modes remain free. Players can choose the option that fits how often they play.

Just Add begins with a craving. Type what you feel like eating, describe it aloud, or take a photo, and the app suggests three meals built around it, adding protein, fiber, and healthy fats to round out the plate.

Shopping support, hands-free cooking, and remembered preferences help carry the idea through to dinner. The winner of Abbey’s Kitchen’s nutrition challenge also impressed judges with a dinner-host feature that accounts for other people’s preferences.

The reply you send every week, the PDF you keep hunting for, and the image you paste repeatedly can all live in SnipStash. It organizes reusable text, images, and files across iPhone, iPad, and Mac, with a keyboard, Mac quick palette, hotkeys, and Shortcuts bringing them within reach.

Private iCloud sync connects the library across devices. Its speed and native interface helped it win Christopher Lawley’s productivity challenge.

Knowing what you want to say and saying it aloud are different things. Rehearse – Hard Conversations gives you an AI counterpart to practice with before a difficult workplace discussion.

Choose from 33 prepared scenarios or bring your own situation, then review feedback on clarity, empathy, assertiveness, and structure. A prep card helps you take the useful points into the real conversation. The app won Leadership Heather’s career-coaching challenge for its focused, straightforward approach.

You see a game in a short video, mean to look it up later, and forget its name. Game Magnet lets you share the clip to identify the games and save them to your backlog.

Processing happens in the background, with a notification when the result is ready. Shelves, ratings, and ranked lists help organize what comes next. The app won Mr Lewis Blogs Gaming’s challenge by making discovery and keeping track of games easier.

FeelGood: Movement Menu starts with a short check-in about your energy, available time, and how you want to feel. It then offers a small menu of movement, with an appetizer, main, and optional sides.

The options span yoga, Pilates, strength, walking, stretching, and breathing. Rest is part of the experience, too. Yusra Salam and Ameena Malik’s app won Simone Sharice’s yoga and fitness challenge, with judges praising its calm, inviting design.

BoatBrawl brings arcade boat battles to a group of up to eight players. Steer, fire, and scramble through interactive arenas, with bots and shared-screen play offering more ways to join in.

Rehaan Cubes and Ashwin K M used Kotlin Multiplatform and Compose Multiplatform to bring the game across phones and computers, including iOS, Android, Mac, Windows, and the web. Its smooth browser play helped it take JetBrains’ Ship Kotlin Everywhere award.

Trazo: Golf Ball Tracer adds a ball-flight line to your own golf videos. Select the ball twice to start, and the processing runs on the phone.

Julian Praest’s app won Most Viral App for the way its team introduced the product through organic content. Golf humor and practical advice set up the tracer reveal, making the finished video part of the pitch. For golfers, the appeal is simple: make the shot easier to follow when you share it.

One Piece Card Game collectors know that similar-looking cards can be very different finds. Grandfolio helps identify artwork variants, manage duplicates, and track collection prices and progress.The Best App for Galaxy winner impressed judges with layouts that adapt to folded, tabletop, and open-screen use. There’s an App Store release for Apple users, too, making this a relevant discovery even if your collection starts on an iPhone.

Let’s Get Support by 3Dadz is designed for partners, parents, and friends affected by someone else’s addiction. Check-ins, journaling, boundary-setting tools, and local resources give those supporters a place to focus on their own needs.

Joshua Burgess and Aaryn B. built the app from community conversations in British Columbia, using Replit to turn the idea into a live product. It won Replit’s Idea to Income award. The announcement links to its Android release.

PoquitoTalk: Bocas del Toro turns English into local Spanish voice notes for WhatsApp and helps translate the replies. The recipient doesn’t need to install the app, keeping the conversation in a tool people already use.

Creator Dorien Van den Abbeele drew on years of life in Bocas del Toro. Its Growth Loop Award recognizes extensive growth experiments and an honest account of what the measurements could show. The announcement links to Google Play.

Parky! combines wait times, dining availability, and memories from Disney and Universal visits. Built by former Disney Cast Member Chase Van Derrick, it aims to offer useful help throughout a park day.

Dining alerts can become conversations on the Lock Screen, where you can ask about another time or restaurant. Alongside queue Live Activities and recap messages, those interactive notifications helped Parky! win the Keep Them Coming Back Award.

In Your Own Words offers short, real-world speaking scenarios and feedback on pace, hesitation, and filler words. Speech analysis takes place on the phone, giving users a way to practice the words before they need them.

Natasha Wilson’s app won the Funnel Vision Award for connecting a web quiz, a relevant practice deck, and a web purchase that unlocks access in the app. The introduction to the product begins before the download.

Friendpire turns the idea of a board-game night with friends into a shared hex-tile world. Gather resources, build farms and docks, and grow an empire together. RevenueCat employee Erik MacInnis built the game to make it easier to stay connected with friends who rarely find time to play.

Friendpire won the separate Conflict of Interest Award, which was open to RevenueCat and sponsor employees and judged by Shipaton participants. It is included here as an optional sidebar and is not part of the 20 first-place count above.

From a useful tool for the workday to a game for your next night in, Shipaton’s winners show how much can come from taking an idea all the way to a shipped app. Explore the winners above, or browse RevenueCat’s full announcement for more apps from the competition.

#10220. Apple MacBook Pro (14-inch, M6) review: Speedy M6 chip slips into comfortable shoes

Join for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

While the base MacBook Pro design is getting a bit tired, Apple’s new M6 chip gives the 14-inch MacBook even more pep in its step.

M6 continues Apple’s track record of solid year-over-year performance upgrades

Memory and SSD upgrades are more expensive than ever before

Design is starting to look a bit stale after 5 years

Why you can trust Tom's Hardware Our expert reviewers spend hours testing and comparing products and services so you can choose the best for you. Find out more about how we test.

It’s that time of the year again, which means that Apple has released yet another refreshed 14-inch MacBook Pro using a legacy chassis. While it looks the same as last year’s M5 models on the outside, it has some key hardware upgrades underneath that address performance and connectivity.

This time around, we have a MacBook Pro using the new M6 chip that was first introduced in the refreshed Mac Mini. In addition, Apple has outfitted the MacBook Pro with Wi-Fi 7 and Bluetooth 6 connectivity courtesy of the in-house Apple N1 chip.

Thanks to industry-wide price hikes on memory and storage, Apple’s 14-inch MacBook Pro now starts at $1,999.

Apple has been using the same design for its entry-level MacBook Pro since 2021, and there are no changes this year (if you want a slightly refreshed design, check out the touch screen-enabled 14- and 16-inch MacBook Pros). It’s still a good-looking laptop, but it’s showing its age. Our MacBook Pro review unit came finished in silver instead of space black. While space black looks great, it’s a fingerprint magnet, so many people might prefer the lighter finish, which better hides your skin oils.

The notch at the top of the display now seems archaic, especially after seeing the Dynamic Island on the pricier MacBook Pros. The notch is always staring back at you, even though I have mostly gotten used to it over the years. The main thing going in the notch’s favor is that it resides in the same space as the menu bar, which limits the visual annoyance.

My only other niggle with the design is one that I’ve harped about in previous MacBook Pro reviews: the cutout in the keyboard deck that aids in lifting the lid has sharp corners, which can jab your palms while typing.

As with previous MacBook Pro generations, you’ll find three Thunderbolt 4 ports (Thunderbolt 5 is not available on M6 models), a full-size SDXC card reader, HDMI 2.1, a 3.5 mm audio jack, and a MagSafe 3 connector.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Our 14-inch MacBook review unit measures 12.31 x 8.71 x 0.61 inches and weighs 3.4 pounds, the same as last year’s model with the M5. For comparison, the Gigabyte Aero X16 measures 14 x 9.9 x 0.78 inches and 4.2 pounds, while the Dell XPS 14 comes in at 12.19 x 8.26 x 0.6 inches and weighs 3 pounds.

14.2-inch Liquid Retina XDR display with Nano-texture option, 3,024 x 1,964, 120 Hz ProMotion

3x Thunderbolt 4 over USB Type-C, HDMI 2.1, SDXC card slot, 3.5 mm headphone jack, MagSafe 3

12.31 x 8.71 x 0.61 inches (312.6 mm x 221.2 mm x 15.1 mm)

Our 14-inch MacBook Pro review unit features a 12-core CPU and 12-core GPU version of Apple’s new M6 SoC. This is the same configuration as the Mac Mini that we reviewed in September, except that our laptop review unit includes 16GB of unified LPDDR5x memory compared to 24GB. Rounding out the specs is a standard 1TB SSD.

Slow and steady wins the race in silicon advances. In every productivity benchmark, our M6 MacBook Pro not only outshone the previous-generation M5 MacBook Pro, but it also trounced the prosumer Gigabyte Aero X16 (AMD Ryzen AI 7 350) and Dell XPS 14 (Intel Core Ultra X7 358H) Windows 11 laptops that we pitted it against.

Starting out with the Geekbench 6 synthetic CPU benchmark, our M6 review unit put up a modest 10% gain in single-core performance over the M5 (4,752 versus 4,288). Switching to multi-core performance, that performance delta grew to just over 18%. Of the two Windows 11 laptops, the XPS 14 came the closest, putting up single- and multi-core scores of 2,867 and 16,927, respectively.

Apple appears to have held the line when it comes to SSD performance year-over-year. 2025's M5 MacBook Pro managed 1917.35 MBps in our in-house 25GB file transfer test. Things changed little this year, as our M6 MacBook Pro came in at 1914.23 MBps. The Aero X16 carried the baton for the Windows 11 field with a result of 1,480.57 MBps.

Our Handbrake test consists of transcoding a 4K video file to 1080p. The new M6 MacBook Pro review finished the job in a brisk 2 minutes and 50 seconds, while the M5 version took 3 minutes and 31 seconds to complete the task.

Our stress test for productivity laptops centers around Cinebench 2026, which we run 10 times back-to-back, tallying multi-core scores along the way. The M6 MacBook Pro started with a score of 5,026.55 and hovered around 5,440 for the remainder of the runs.

The M5 includes a 12-core GPU (each core features a dedicated neural accelerator), which promises up to a 30 percent uplift in performance compared to the M5.

Although the base MacBook Pro isn’t what we’d consider a gaming beast, it did manage 53.7 frames per second (FPS) at 1800 x 1125 resolution in Cyberpunk 2077 using the “For this Mac” preset and 32.66 FPS at 3024 x 1890. This compares to 47.65 FPS and 31.47 FPS, respectively, for the M5 MacBook Pro.

Apple’s M6-equipped 14-inch MacBook Pro continues to use an IPS display with mini LED backlighting. As before, you get a 3,024 x 1,964 resolution panel with a 120 Hz refresh rate.

While the mini LED panel looks good, it’s not nearly as capable as the touch-enabled PROLED XDR display found on its pricier siblings. Although you can equip the MacBook Pro with a nano-texture display for $150, our review unit didn’t have it.

Being that I’m a huge fan of Sylvester Stallone’s Rocky films (well, the first four), I pulled up the second trailer for I Play Rocky, which is set to release later this month. The design aesthetic of the film is, of course, distinctly 1970s, and the mini LED panel did a good job of presenting the muted colors (mostly warm earth tones).

As I’m still in shock over the passing of Patrick Stewart, I watched a sizable portion of Star Trek: First Contact on the screen. Again, the display performed well, with major kudos during the opening battle scene where Federation ships are battling the Borg. The orange explosions, red phaser blasts, and the Borg’s green cutting beams vividly danced across the screen. The blackness of space wasn’t quite as deep as I witnessed with an OLED screen side-by-side, but it’s still good.

Our MacBook Pro review unit was even brighter than last year’s model, if only by a slim margin (571.4 nits versus 558 nits). That performance was still well ahead of the OLED panels in the Windows laptops.

Those similarities extended to color performance, which tracked nearly identically to the preceding M5 MacBook Pro, which was a dead ringer for its M4 predecessor. Our instrumented tests showed 81.5 percent coverage of DCI-P3 and 115.1 percent of the sRGB. Although the XPS 14’s OLED panel was no match for brightness (358 nits), it pipped the MacBook Pro in color performance (89.7 percent DCI-P3, 126.6 percent sRGB).

As with all the other exterior design elements of the M6 MacBook Pro, the keyboard remains unchanged. To me, that’s a good thing, as it’s one of the things I love the most about Apple laptops. As someone who lived through Apple’s horrible butterfly keys on the 12-inch MacBook and subsequent MacBook Pro models, the return to the venerable switches was a return to sanity on Apple’s part.

Apple isn’t the type of company that would equip its laptops with RGB keyboards, so you’ll get single-zone white LED key backlighting. Although the thought of a MacBook Pro with RGB lighting brings a slight smirk to my face, I’m perfectly fine with Apple’s monochromatic approach.

I used the keyhero.com typing test, as I do with all laptops I review, and settled in with 93 words per minute at 95 percent accuracy. Those figures track with what I achieve with other Apple and Windows laptops.

Like all MacBooks (except for the entry-level Neo), our MacBook Pro features a large haptic trackpad that measures 5 x 3 inches. The glass surface makes gliding with your finger or thumb easy-peasy, and the simulated “click” is satisfying.

We’re sure you’re noticing a theme in this review, but the audio system in our MacBook Pro review unit remains unchanged compared to its earlier M5 and M4 counterparts. What that means is that you’ll find a six-speaker system that includes support for major audio formats like Dolby Digital, Dolby Atmos, Dolby Digital Plus, and Apple Spatial Audio.

I’m a sucker for acoustic guitar, and one of the greats of the 2000s is John Mayer. Perhaps my favorite John Mayer song is “3x5” from his debut album, which my girlfriend at the time (now wife) and I played over and over again and witnessed several times in concert. On the MacBook Pro’s speakers, the acoustic guitar packs just as much of a punch as John’s breathy vocals pelting out vivid lyrics like, "Today, skies are painted colors of a cowboy cliché."

I got so lost in the audio experience that I found myself listening to the entire Room for Squares album and being transported back to my college days.

While most Windows laptops give you some semblance of upgradeability, allowing you to upgrade at the very least storage, Wi-Fi/Bluetooth, and sometimes memory, you’ll find no such luxuries in MacBook land. There are four pentalobe screws on the bottom of the laptop, but once you remove the cover, there’s nothing of note that’s upgradeable. The M6 chip with its integrated 16GB of memory can’t be replaced, nor can the 1TB SSD – both packages are soldered onto the motherboard. As with all MacBooks, you’ll need to settle on how much RAM and storage you want at purchase time. If you get it wrong, you’ll have to live with that config until it’s time to upgrade your laptop again.

If there’s one thing that you can count on when it comes to MacBooks, it’s battery life. It’s pretty common for MacBooks to last for 18 or more hours in our battery life test, which consists of browsing the web, running OpenGL tests, and streaming video at 150 nits of brightness.

Our MacBook Pro review unit was no exception, lasting 19 hours and 5 minutes thanks to its 72 WHr battery. This was nearly a one-hour improvement over its M5 counterpart, which is a testament to the efficiency gains that Apple is able to achieve with the 2-nanometer process node.

We measured skin temperature on the MacBook Pro during our Cinebench 2026 stress test. Unlike the cheaper MacBook Airs, which are fanless, the MacBook Pro’s two internal fans kick into gear quite quickly to dissipate heat.

The hottest point was near the left exhaust vent, which measured 106 degrees Fahrenheit, with 105 F seen between the G and H keys. Given its distance from the toasty parts of the laptop, the touchpad remained cool to the touch at 78 F. The touchpad was much cooler at 82.8 F.

We use TG Pro to measure internal temperatures while running the Cinebench 2026 stress test. While TG Pro doesn’t have full support for the M6 yet, the Power Manager dies reported peak temperatures of 80 degrees Celsius (C) for the SoC. The overall system temperature, which takes an average of several components (i.e., power supply, battery, SSD, logic board, etc.), was 43 degrees C.

The 12MP Center Stage camera that’s been featured in the last two MacBook Pro generations has made a reappearance this year. All of the superlatives that I had for the M5 MacBook Pro are relevant to my current review unit. Namely, image quality was impressive across the board, in both bright and low-light conditions.

Unlike some other laptop webcams that skew my darker skin color or apply smoothing algorithms that make my skin look like a Tootsie Roll, the camera picked up the wrinkles around my eyes and accurately represented the grays in my beard.

The M6 MacBook Pro ships with macOS 27 Golden Gate preinstalled. You can liken the shift from macOS 26 Tahoe to macOS 27 Golden Gate to the OS X 10.5 Leopard to Mac OS X 10.6 Snow Leopard transition. In other words, macOS 27 is more about making the operating system more reliable and faster rather than bombarding users with new features. You can read more about macOS 27 Golden Gate in my M6 Mac Mini review.

Just like Windows 11, macOS comes with a slew of apps preinstalled upon first boot. Some of the standouts include GarageBand, Apple’s productivity suite (Keynote, Pages, Numbers), Apple TV, iMovie, and Home (Apple’s smart home application). With the exception of Apple TV, the other apps are productivity utilities meant to enhance the user experience and get work done. You won’t find typical bloatware like app/shortcuts for services like Dropbox or antivirus programs like McAfee as you would on Windows 11 laptops.

When opting for the 14-inch MacBook Pro with M6 processor, you’re looking at a starting price of $1,999. In addition to the M6 processor, you get 16GB of RAM and a 1TB SSD (the configuration of our review unit).

From there, you can opt for 24GB (+$200) or 32AM (+$400) of RAM, and 2TB (+$500) or 4TB (+$1,500) of storage. There are also two standalone options that you can add: the nano-texture display for $150 and a 96-watt power adapter for an extra $20.

The MacBook Pro has a standard one-year warranty. However, you can pick up AppleCare+ for $99.99/year or $9.99/month. The coverage adds unlimited repairs covering accidents, 24/7 priority tech support, and battery replacement.

When it comes to everything you see on the outside, Apple’s latest 14-inch MacBook Pro is more of the same: same display, same chassis, same keyboard, and the same great build quality. But true to form, Apple has steadily focused on improving performance year-over-year, and we see that again with the new M6 SoC.

You’ll see anywhere from a 10% to 20% uplift across the board, depending on whether you’re dealing with single- or multi-threaded apps. On top of that, efficiency gains have netted nearly an additional hour of battery life over its M5-powered predecessor. And Apple has finally given the MacBook Pro Wi-Fi 7 and Bluetooth 6.0 support.

The M6 MacBook Pro doesn’t have the OLED touchscreen, more svelte design, or Dynamic Island of the all-new MacBook Pros, but it also started at a price that is $900 less than those systems. With that said, the $1,999 price of our review unit is still a lot of money to ask for a laptop featuring just 16GB of RAM and a 1TB SSD. And you don’t even get an OLED screen at that price.

But given the price of memory and storage these days, $1,999 isn’t exactly out of the ordinary. A Dell XPS 14 configured with an Intel Core Ultra 5 325, 16GB of RAM, 1TB SSD, and a 2.8K OLED display is $2,549. We are truly living in unprecedented times, and while it’s hard to reset our expectations concerning the price of tech, the M6 MacBook Pro remains a solid entry in a highly competitive market.

Brandon Hill is a senior editor at Tom's Hardware. He has written about PC and Mac tech since the late 1990s with bylines at AnandTech, DailyTech, and Hot Hardware. When he is not consuming copious amounts of tech news, he can be found enjoying the NC mountains or the beach with his wife and two sons.

#10217. Google’s AI search fumbles leave schools in the lurch

Good morning, and happy Ocarina of Time day to all who celebrate. Oh, and speaking of celebrating: congratulations to the real winners of the midterms, YouTube and Twitch streamers.

Inbox is written by Daniel Bader. Read previous issues here, and then catch up with Ben Schoon’s excellent Weekender column published every Sunday.

It’s hard to overstate how reliant US school districts are on Google. Almost every school board in the country purchases Chromebooks for their students, many of whom are able to take them home and treat them as personal laptops. IT and procurement managers like them because they’re relatively cheap and offer zero-touch enrollment, which lowers upfront and ongoing maintenance costs. Teachers also like them because Google Classroom is relatively intuitive and ChromeOS is optimized for most other web-based education platforms.

But this reliance on Google’s tech stack comes at a cost: school boards are forced to comply with the company’s capricious terms of service, in particular when it comes to the creeping proliferation of AI across its products.

Things came to a head earlier this week when the New York Times reported that changes to the Google Search experience in schools will require opting into Gemini, Gemini Notebook, Gemini in Classroom, and, perhaps most importantly, AI Overviews. The move actually comes after Google was pressured to make student AI usage opt-in. Google’s justification: search and AI are now “deeply integrated” in the tech stack, so disabling one means disabling the other.

Google’s alternative to AI Overviews is a new Guided Learning feature, which replaces search’s primary function — solving a problem — with a step-by-step guide that helps a student understand said problem.

Former FTC Chair Lina Khan took to X to decry the move: “Google is using its search monopoly to coerce schools into adopting AI,” she said. “This highlights the danger of allowing an unregulated monopolist to control an essential service. Multiple courts have found Google liable for violating antitrust laws, and this latest move shows it remains unchastened. As the public increasingly pushes back against the use of AI in schools, Google is making clear it will punish those who don’t fall in line.”

I have a feeling Google will need to walk back this decision in some form. As 9to5’s Ben Schoon points out, it’s comically easy to disable AI Overviews in the current version of Google Search, and it doesn’t seem to have a detrimental impact on the subsequent results. But Google is likely worried that if it is forced to carve out versions of the search experience for specific user bases, revenue from its burgeoning AI Overviews ad business may be impacted.

Even if Google sticks to its proverbial guns, the move exposes a rift between the public, particularly parents, and the tech giants pushing AI on their users. Furthermore, the AI boom has had a direct impact on educators’ ability to purchase new hardware and upgrade old Chromebook fleets, since component prices have increased by as much as 5x over the past year. Some lower-income districts are even recalling Chromebooks from students, reverting to a shared laptop pool to save money.

For a much less complicated relationship with Google Search, try its Ocarina of Time Easter egg. Search for “Zelda” or “Ocarina of Time,” then tap the Master Sword to travel back to a 1998-style results page, complete with Link’s sound effects. It’s a trip down memory lane to a time when, as even Google acknowledges, things were a whole lot simpler.

Was AI on the ballot? According to Ben Thompson at Stratechery, it was in a big way. Democrats and Republicans both rejected data centers, but Democrats want to actually pass some regulation reining in the hyperscalers’ and frontier labs’ ambitions.

First we were short on compute, but that ramped; now we are short on memory, but that will ramp; we were coming close to being short on power, but that will be rectified. The final constraint is political, and that may be the most imposing constraint of all. Not only is it harder to solve, but it actually interferes with solving the other constraints. Many of the theories about AI assume that it becomes a commodity; I think it’s just as likely we are looking at scarcity for much longer than we expected, which will have ripple effects on everything.

Is it possible to pass broad AI regulation that will slow down an industry that has been allowed to run unchecked since Trump’s reelection in 2024? And is it in the best interest of these companies to support it, even if it means a short-term slowdown at a time when the two biggest AI labs, Anthropic and OpenAI, are about to IPO?

Some fascinating developments in the $6.3 billion Binance hack: turns out it was yet another case of social engineering. A Binance engineer was lured into a fake job interview, before which he downloaded malware that let hackers breach his computer and, eventually, others on the network.

After my discussion with Will last week about the death of browsers, it came as no surprise to learn that Brave is being sued by the New York Times for copyright infringement. Brave is accused of scraping paywalled material from sites like nytimes.com and selling access to it through a search API to AI companies, which use it as training data. Yes, it’s difficult to earn money as a browser maker (see Arc for another compelling example), but this is particularly egregious, even for a company familiar with shady business practices.

You can also support us by becoming a 9to5Google Pro member, starting at just $5/month. You get bonus podcast episodes, access to our members-only Discord server, and lots more.

Thanks for reading, and have a great day. I’ll talk to you on Tuesday.

#10216. Why Your AI Agent Can't Read the Web (and How to Build a Fetch Layer That Can)

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

This is the least glamorous part of building with LLMs and it's where a lot of agent projects quietly fall over. The model is rarely the problem. The fetch is. In this article we'll look at why live web retrieval has become so unreliable for AI tools, what the numbers say about the shift, and how to build a small, well-behaved fetch layer in Python that fails loudly instead of feeding your model garbage.

A few years ago, a plain requests.get() worked on most of the public web. That's no longer true, and the reason is simple: there are now more bots than people.

According to the 2025 Imperva Bad Bot Report, automated traffic made up 51% of all web traffic in 2024. That's the first time in a decade it has passed human activity. Bad bots alone (scrapers, credential stuffers, scalpers) went from 32% to 37% of traffic in a single year.

Source: Imperva Bad Bot Report 2024 and 2025

From a site owner's point of view, your polite research agent and a credential-stuffing botnet look much the same at the network edge. Both are non-browser clients making fast requests from data-center IP ranges. So the defaults have tightened. In July 2025 Cloudflare began blocking known AI crawlers by default for new customers, and bot management products now look at TLS fingerprints, header order, IP reputation and JavaScript execution, not just the User-Agent string.

It helps to understand the publisher's side of the deal, because it explains why your agent is being treated with suspicion.

Cloudflare splits AI crawling into three purposes: training (collecting data to train models), search (building an index for AI search answers) and user action (fetching a page because a person just asked a question). Training dominates.

Training crawls take content and send nothing back. Cloudflare measures this with a crawl-to-referral ratio: how many pages a platform crawls for every visitor it sends to the site. In its crawl-to-click gap analysis, the ratios for some AI platforms ran into the tens of thousands, compared with single digits for traditional search.

If you ran a publication and saw those numbers, you'd block too. The catch is that blocking is usually done by user agent or IP range, so it hits user-action fetches as well. Those are the ones that happen when a person asks your agent to read a specific article, and Cloudflare's 2025 Year in Review found they grew more than 15x during the year. Your agent is caught in a fight it didn't start.

The mix of crawlers also shifts quickly, which makes static allow and block lists fragile on both sides:

And restrictions keep piling up. The Consent in Crisis study audited 14,000 web domains and found that a large and fast-growing share of the sources behind the C4 training corpus now restrict AI crawlers, either through robots.txt or their terms of service.

Source: Longpre et al., Consent in Crisis (NeurIPS 2024)

So that's the environment your agent fetches in. Here's how to build for it.

Before writing any retry logic, put names on your failures. "The request failed" is useless to both you and the model. In practice almost every failed fetch falls into one of four buckets:

Bucket 2 is the dangerous one, because a block page often comes back with a 200 OK. If you don't check, the model will happily summarise "Checking your browser before accessing..." as if it were the article.

Here's a compact fetch layer in Python using httpx and the standard library's robots.txt parser. It's around 100 lines and covers the four buckets above.

Use a descriptive user agent with a contact URL. It costs you nothing and site operators are far more likely to allow a client they can identify and reach. Then check robots.txt (standardised as RFC 9309) before every new host.

The lru_cache means you fetch robots.txt once per origin rather than once per page. In a long-running service you'll want a cache with a TTL instead, since robots.txt files do change.

This is the core of the layer. Instead of returning raw HTML, return a result that says which bucket you landed in.

The marker list is deliberately simple. It won't catch every interstitial, but it catches the common ones, and a false "blocked" is much cheaper than a confident summary of a CAPTCHA page. Log every blocked result with its first few hundred characters so you can grow the list from real data.

Retries should depend on the bucket. Throttled and broken responses get exponential backoff with jitter. Blocked and not-allowed responses get no retries at all.

Note the cap on wait. A site sending Retry-After: 3600 is telling you to go away for an hour. An interactive agent should report that to the user rather than block a request thread for that long.

The cheapest fetch is the one you don't make. Agents often re-read the same documentation page several times in one session. Store the ETag and Last-Modified headers and send them back as If-None-Match and If-Modified-Since. A 304 Not Modified response costs the server almost nothing and lowers the odds of being rate limited.

Wire these into fetch() by passing headers=conditional_headers(url) to client.get(). On a 304, return the cached text. On a 200, call remember().

Finally, don't hide failures from the LLM. Give the tool output a structured shape so the model can reason about it:

(extract_main_text is whatever readability or boilerplate-removal step you already use. Libraries like trafilatura work well here.)

With this in place, the model's answer changes from a made-up summary to "I couldn't read that page because the site blocks automated access. Here's what I found from two other sources." That's a better product, and it builds trust with users.

Once you've done everything above, some legitimate user-action fetches will still fail. That's usually because the site blocks data-center IP ranges outright, not because of anything your agent did. Teams handle this in a few ways, roughly in order of preference:

The principle is the same in every case: the network path is a reliability decision, not a way around a site's wishes. Your robots.txt check and your honest user agent should stay in place whichever route the request takes.

Once your fetch layer returns structured results, observability is easy. Emit one event per fetch with the host, status bucket and latency, and watch these:

If you're prototyping agents with AI coding assistants, ask them to generate these metrics hooks from the start. They're easy to bolt on now and painful to add later. For a sense of how well current tools handle this kind of infrastructure code, Vibe Coder Journal publishes hands-on reviews and benchmarks of AI coding tools.

The web your agent reads in 2026 isn't the open, fetch-anything web many of us learned on. More than half the traffic is automated, publishers have good reasons to be wary of AI crawlers, and the defaults now lean toward blocking. You can't change that. You can make your agent a good citizen, though: one that identifies itself, respects the rules, backs off when asked, and tells the truth when it can't read something.

Most of the reliability gains come from the unglamorous parts: classifying failures, caching and passing honest errors to the model. Get those right and your agent will feel much more dependable, even on the days when half the web won't let it in.

It depends on the jurisdiction, the site's terms of service and what you do with the content. Respecting robots.txt and site terms is the baseline. For commercial products, get proper legal advice.

Many site owners expect it to, and RFC 9309 makes no exception for user-triggered fetches. The safest default is to obey it and tell the user when a page is disallowed.

Many bot-protection systems serve a challenge page with a 200 status. Check the body for challenge markers before passing it to the model, as in the looks_blocked() function above.

Only for pages that render their main content with JavaScript. Try a plain HTTP fetch first. Headless browsers are slower, cost more to run and are easier to fingerprint.

#10214. Apple MacBook Pro with PROLED XDR Display review: The screen that every Mac deserves

Join for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.
