The MacBook Pro with PROLED XDR display has the best laptop screen I've ever seen, and other changes like reducing weight and moving from the notch to the Dynamic Island add to an excellent, albeit very pricey, machine.

Dynamic Island is far more useful than notch

Why you can trust Tom's Hardware Our expert reviewers spend hours testing and comparing products and services so you can choose the best for you. Find out more about how we test.

Apple's fans know all about waiting. The company tends to polish features, especially in hardware, that some other laptop vendors release faster. The MacBook Pro with PROLED XDR Display ends the wait for two long-requested elements: OLED and touch, both on the platform for the very first time.

That comes at a price. The 16-inch version we have on review starts at $3,399 and is $7,899 as configured. 14-inch systems start at $2,899. Those are $400 bumps over the existing MacBook Pros with identical chips, making the OLED panel a pricey upgrade.

But those who can afford these high-end systems will get the best display on a Mac, if not laptops in general. Add in a significant reduction in weight and improved speakers, and you're getting a lot of small tweaks that add up over what was already one of the best laptops, even if many of the aesthetics haven't changed.

From a distance, this new MacBook Pro doesn't look all that different from the existing models, which are still on the market, which may upset some people looking for a more sweeping redesign. They come in the same colors (space black, like our review unit, and silver) and they largely have the same design. Technically, the new OLED display stack allows for a thinner system, but our 16-inch review unit, at 16 mm thick, is just 0.8 mm shorter than an equivalent 16-inch MacBook Pro with Liquid Retina screen.

The aesthetic differences show up once you open the laptop. For one, the notch in the display is gone with the PROLED XDR models, replaced with the Dynamic Island from the latest iPhones. Let us rejoice; while the notch never bothered me on phones, it always felt in the way, dipping into the macOS menu bar. This isn't perfect, but it feels far more natural and has added functionality (see "Dynamic Island and macOS Touch Features" below).

Then there's the glowing Apple logo on the back of the lid. We haven't seen this for years, and while I could take or leave it, it's definitely going to make some fans nostalgic. The old glowing Mac logos used light from LCD backlights, getting the effect with no extra power. Because OLED panels only light up pixels when they're needed, Apple is making an aesthetic choice to power the logo with a bit of extra electricity this time. The glow matches the brightness of the display, which is a fun touch. I didn't see anything in settings to disable it.

Apple's bezels are thinner, which has let Apple stretch the display to 16.3 inches on this new 16-inch model (it's still 14.2 inches on the smaller one). The displays are now rounded on all four sides. On the Liquid Retina displays, they're rounded on top to match the curves in the chassis, but are squared on the bottom. It's not a huge change, but it does cut some pixels on the bottom of the screen and looks a bit like an iPad.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Otherwise, the system looks largely the same. You have the same ports: MagSafe 3, two Thunderbolt 5 ports, and a 3.5 mm headphone jack on the left; and an SD card reader, an additional Thunderbolt port, and HDMI output on the right.

But the OLED laptop feels different when you pick it up or put it in a backpack. Changes to cooling in the new 16-inch MacBook Pro with PROLED XDR and the switch to an OLED screen get the laptop down to 4.1 pounds with M5 Max (4.0 pounds with M5 Pro), whereas the Liquid Retina versions are 4.7 pounds. You can really feel the half-pound difference. (The 14-incher is down from 3.5 pounds to 3 pounds, too.)

And that's lighter than some of its new competition, too. Microsoft's Surface Laptop Ultra with Nvidia's RTX Spark chip is 4.4 pounds and thicker at 0.75 inches. The Lenovo Pro 9n, also with RTX Spark and its own OLED screen, is a lighter 3.64 pounds and is thicker at 16.7 mm.

There has been talk of a sturdier hinge, but it does still wobble when you touch it. No one has gotten around that issue. Comparing it to my personal MacBook Pro with the old design, the display goes back ever so slightly more, which is nice for touch but not a game-changer. It would be nice to see a 180-degree hinge for people who really want to write or draw on a flat surface (Apple Pencil support would be nice for that, too). For now, people who want that will need to stick to an iPad.

I spent some of the time reviewing this laptop thinking of Microsoft's Surface Laptop Studio, a touch-focused workstation with a hinge that lets the screen sit in three positions: a standard laptop, pulled forward over the keyboard, or lying like a tablet. That didn't succeed (neither did similar designs from Acer), but it took a stab at changing how a touch-based laptop worked. The new MacBook Pro design isn't a radical rethink of the ergonomics that have plagued touch laptops for over a decade. It's just a powerful Mac that happens to have a touchscreen.

Apple M5 Max (18 cores: 6 super cores, 12 performance cores)

16.3-inch tandem OLED display (PROLED XDR), 3472-by-2266 native resolution, 254 PPI, touch, True Tone, ProMotion (VRR 10-120 Hz), Standard glass

Apple N1 with Wi-Fi 7, Bluetooth 6, Thread

3x Thunderbolt 5 (USB-C), 3.5 mm headphone jack, SD card reader, HDMI, MagSafe 3

12MP Center Stage camera (1080p HD video recording)

14 x 9.62 x 0.63 inches (355.5 x 244.2 x 16 mm)

We had to wait a long time to get OLED on a Mac. PROLED XDR is a silly name, but I'll give Apple this: The screen is absolutely stunning. It's the best screen I've ever seen on a notebook, and it makes me jealous when I look back at my personal system with plain old mini LED. Everything from the default wallpaper in macOS Golden Gate to videos and photos pop on this display. The blacks are deep, the colors pop, and it can get intensely bright.

Our review unit came with the 16.3-inch (slightly enlarged thanks to thinner bezels from 16.2-inches on non-PROLED models) with 3472-by-2266 native resolution, a variable refresh rate of 10 - 120 Hz, and, of course, touch support.

I used the PROLED XDR display to watch the finale of the latest season of Slow Horses. In one scene, where the mastermind of the season's plot shows up to a restaurant to meet with the head of MI5, I was wowed by the deep blacks. The restaurant was sparsely lit, and both characters were wearing black coats. The display brought out the highlights in the environment, as well as a few red accent walls.

In our instrumented testing, the PROLED XDR screen covered 83.6% of the DCI-P3 color gamut by volume and 118% sRGB, actually slightly lower than the mini LED version. I've seen them side-by-side. Trust my eyes; there's no contest here.

The MacBook Pro with PROLED XDR measured 542.6 nits in our SDR brightness test, falling below the Surface Laptop Ultra, with its near-blinding 944 nits with its mini LED display. But again, PROLED simply looks better. The Surface Laptop Ultra has some light bleed and not enough local dimming zones to catch up.

The OLED panel on the Lenovo Pro 9n is the most vivid on each gamut by volume. I think it's a bit oversaturated, but if you prefer extreme pops of color, you may prefer that screen. The tandem OLED on the Dell XPS 16 was also more vivid on measurements, but was far less bright, which mutes the best aspects of OLED.

The PROLED screen showed how bright it could get with HDR content, outputting 1,964 nits in our 40% HDR test.

While I love how the display looks out of the box, Apple is offering more reference modes than ever before on a laptop, including various video, cinema, web and photography standards, and even DICOM medical imaging at 350 and 600 nits. There's also a display calibrator to fine-tune white points or perform full recalibration with external measurement tools.

Apple is far from the first company to add OLED (or, in this case, tandem OLED) to a laptop, but this stands out among the best OLED laptop displays I've seen in recent memory. The price hike to get it — $400 over mini LED models with the same chips, will be out of reach for many (though that hike also shaves half a pound off the weight). I desperately hope Apple starts bringing its brand of OLED down to the rest of the MacBook Pro and MacBook Air line, too. After all, the iPad Pro and iPad Mini both have it.

We tested the MacBook Pro with a standard screen. While I haven't spent significant time with a PROLED with the matte nano-texture display, I can tell you that this looks so good with a glossy finish that I'm not sure I could risk letting the matte displays (which I usually love!) take away any of the color saturation or context on display here. And as far as reflections go, they were minimal, even next to a window.

Outside of OLED, there are two other additions to the MacBook Pro's screen.

The first is the Dynamic Island, a group of black pixels around the punch-hole webcam that can be used to show system alerts, notifications, and ongoing activities. (If you plug in a monitor, Dynamic Island activities show up to the left of your menu bar apps and icons.) It matches modern iPhones, and it's a huge change over the notch in other Macs, which took up far too much space in the menu bar. Now, the area around the camera is at least useful, though the camera still doesn't offer Face ID.

One of my biggest annoyances with the old design was that your mouse could fall behind the notch, with no indicator of where it was. The mouse can still move behind the Dynamic Island, but now the black area grows to indicate where the pointer is, and even wiggles if you make very small movements, to remind you where the cursor is.

The other much-discussed addition is the touchscreen. Apple hasn't reinvented the wheel here. Like most Windows and Chromebook makers, it added touch functionality onto a standard laptop. Apple's announcement mentions that the "touch sensor is built directly into the display panel — not layered on top of it," suggesting the use of technology similar to the iPhone and flagship Windows laptops.

Apple has made slight adjustments to macOS 27 Golden Gate to accommodate touch. My personal favorite is that the "traffic lights" for maximizing, minimizing, or closing a window expand when you tap on them, to ensure you can hit the right one. When you click on a menu with your finger, the spacing is larger for easier touch targets. Like the iPhone, you can swipe down from the dynamic Island to invoke Siri. You can also flick windows to the sides to organize your windows, though you can already do that with a mouse or keyboard.

Many gestures that work on the trackpad also work on the touchscreen, like pinch to zoom, accessing Mission Control (swiping up with three fingers), showing Notification Center (swiping left from the edge of the screen), and moving between virtual desktops by swiping left or right with three fingers. Many apps have already been optimized for touch, but if they haven't, touch translates to a click, so it all still works fairly well.

One of my many experiments with the touch screen included swiping across the timeline in Final Cut Pro. But in a sample project I put together, I didn't find it any better than using the keyboard and mouse — just another option. In Maxon's Cinema 4D, I felt the same way moving a model car and adjusting virtual cameras. It's an option, but it's no better than the existing way of doing things.

Perhaps my favorite use was in Blender, making adjustments to a 3D model I was sculpting. You could do everything I did with a mouse, but some features, like changing the angle of the sculpture, felt very natural with touch. That being said, after a few minutes, my arm was tired. I couldn't see using this all the time, as there are no changes to ergonomics over the usual laptop design.

For those who never want to use touch, you can turn it off in disability settings. That being said, as someone who would likely only use it for occasional scrolls, I don't see the harm in having it on, even if I don't see myself ever pulling to refresh on Safari when CMD + R is right there. (I do wish Apple included the polishing cloth with the glossy screen on our review unit and not just matte screens, however. Touch screens get fingerprints!).

I found touch most useful for iPad apps on the App Store that work natively on Mac. For instance, my go-to podcast app is Overcast, which is great on my iPhone but designed for touch on Mac. While I'm very used to navigating it with a mouse, the choice to use touch made sense here. It's also great for the iPhone simulator in Xcode, where developers can try out touch-based apps on their touchscreen Mac instead of using a pointer. The same goes for iPhone Mirroring.

There's no touch tutorial. Touch is clearly a feature on these new MacBook Pros, but not the feature. Keyboard and mouse are clearly still king.

The six-speaker sound system in the 16-inch MacBook Pro's new design, complete with updated woofers and tweeters, keeps the crown in Apple’s camp for the best laptop speakers I've ever heard.

When I listened to Justice's "Flower in a Hurricane" with the MacBook Pro, I was floored by the drums. While the rest of the speakers don't sound superbly better than the existing Pros, the low-end has improved, and deep drums and bass easily filled my apartment with deep, well-rounded sound. Everything sounded incredible, from the audio to the synths. This may be the best way I've heard this song since I've had it on a loop after its debut in a Grand Theft Auto VI trailer.

Apple has made some adjustments to how the speaker sounds with the lid shut, too. It's surprisingly good, with The Beaches' cover of "I Ran (So Far Away)" getting surprisingly loud, with the laptop plugged into a Thunderbolt dock and the lid shut, though the drums in that song weren't as snappy. Everything was slightly dampened until I opened the lid, but honestly, I could use it like this. It's still better than many other laptop speakers, with better bass.

We tested the new MacBook Pro with an M5 Max, 128GB of unified memory, and a 2TB SSD. That's the same chip and RAM as in the 14-inch MacBook Pro we tested earlier this year, though that had a 4TB drive.

The main competitors we looked at featured Nvidia's RTX Spark N1X chips, including the Surface Laptop Ultra (20-core version) and the Lenovo Pro 9n (18-core version). We also tossed in the Dell XPS 16 with a Core X7 358H — another 16-inch system with a Tandem OLED.

Unsurprisingly, this MacBook Pro can tear through most people's everyday workloads. Apple has re-tooled the cooling in this system, and it was often close to, if not ahead of, the M5 Max we tested earlier this year in a 14-inch chassis.

On Geekbench 6, the 16-inch MacBook Pro with PROLED XDR earned a score of 4,353 and a multi-core score of 29,430, coming close to the other M5 Max machine. Both of those scores are far ahead of the RTX Spark laptops and the Intel-based Dell.

The 2TB SSD copied roughly 25GB of files at a rate of 1,917.69 MBps, which is fast, though the 4TB SSD in the MacBook Pro we tested earlier this year was far faster. The Surface Laptop Ultra was ever-so-slightly ahead at 1,998.59 MBps.

On Handbrake, the 16-inch MacBook Pro transcoded a 4K video to 1080p in 1 minute and 43 seconds, the fastest of the bunch.

On the Xcode benchmark, in which Macs compile large code bases, the new 16-inch MacBook Pro beat the older 14-inch model with the same M5 Max by 7 seconds, completing the task in 80 seconds.

Our Cinebench 2026 stress test has systems go through ten runs of multi-core rendering, which is particularly stressful. In the automatic performance mode, Apple's default out of the box on macOS 27, the system started at a score of 9,080, before eventually settling in the 8,050's due to throttling. With high performance mode, which runs the fans harder and louder, the Mac started at a score of 9,647 and was far more stable, with its lowest score (9,479) at the very end. If you need to get some tough work done faster, switching to high performance mode could help, as long as you're OK with fan noise.

The MacBook Pro with PROLED XDR display came with the same 40-core M5 Max GPU as the MacBook Pro we reviewed earlier this year.

In the new chassis, the M5 Max scored 4,355 on 3DMark Steel Nomad, beating the old system (3,865) as well as both configurations of the RTX Spark.

Running Cyberpunk 2077's benchmark at 1920 x 1200 using our typical test settings (Ray tracing ultra), the game ran at 36.24 frames per second. This is slightly faster than the 14-inch we tested earlier this year with the existing chassis, at 30.84 FPS. With the "For This Mac" preset, including MetalFX and a resolution of 2316 x 1448, the benchmark ran at 94.20 FPS.

Of all the things that haven't changed on the MacBook Pro, the most familiar are the keyboard and touchpad. That may be of some comfort to stalwarts of indirect input methods.

I was blazing fast on the keyboard, hitting 131 words per minute with a 99% accuracy rate on the monkeytype.com typing test. Both stats are on the high side for me, but I also can't blame Apple for making a comfortable keyboard with chiclet keys and a smart layout. I will praise any company that uses an inverted T for arrow keys as long as I live. Apple may not offer the deepest travel of the laptop market, but it does the job.

The massive trackpad features smooth glass, comfortable haptics, and intuitive gesture support in macOS. The 16-inch MacBook Pro's touchpad is luxuriously huge. After years of Windows laptops adding haptic trackpads to catch up, Apple's touchpad still feels great.

While there are just four Torx Screws on the bottom of the MacBook Pro, there isn't much in the way of upgrading. Apple puts the RAM in the chip, and the storage is soldered down. If you need a repair, your best bet is probably Apple's Genius Bar.

Like other Macs, you should make sure you get the spec you need when you buy. Though, as you'll see in the configuration section, that could cost you quite a bit for some RAM and storage upgrades. Unfortunately, that's not unusual given the price of memory in the greater industry right now.

Apple's 99.6 WHr battery allows for a longer-lasting OLED laptop than its competitors, but in our testing, mini-LED model still lasts longer. On our battery test, laptops browse the web, run OpenGL tests, and stream video with the display set to 150 nits of brightness.

The 16-inch MacBook Pro with the PROLED XDR display lasted 15 hours and 57 minutes, far ahead of the OLED screen on the Lenovo Pro 9n (10:30) and the tandem OLED panel on the Dell XPS 16 (13:03). It also beat the mini-LED screen on the Surface Laptop Ultra.

The nearly 16-hour runtime is slightly short of Apple's "up to 17 hours" claim for web browsing. The only laptop that did better on our battery test was the 14-inch M5 Max laptop we tested earlier this year. That machine, with a smaller, mini-LED screen, ran for just shy of 18 hours.

The MacBook Pro can get a bit toasty under heavy load, though it's nothing I would deem unusable. Heavy workloads are probably best on a desk, though. New cooling changes the direction of the airflow in the machine, moving it out and through the back rather than out from vents near the display, in order to extend the display's lifespan.

During our Cinebench 2026 stress test, the keyboard measured 106.6 degrees Fahrenheit, which is definitely hot enough to notice. The touchpad was a cooler 88 F, while the hottest point on the bottom measured 107 F.

Our usual monitoring was not able to identify the heat on the CPU yet (this may require some software updates), but the overall temperature from the computer's other sensors was an average of 51 degrees Celsius.

The 12-megapixel Center Stage webcam on the MacBook Pro will serve you well even on the most professional call. It looked great in meetings, even in one difficult lighting scenario when I had a lamp on one side and post-Daylight Savings Time early evening light coming in from a window next to me. The system gracefully balanced the mix of warm yellow and the products of blue hour.

I could easily smooth that mix out using the MacBook Pro camera's studio light or edge light effects, too.

We tested this new MacBook Pro with macOS 27 Golden Gate, which we highlighted on the Mac Studio, with new Apple Intelligence features, and the new Siri based on Apple Foundation Models and Gemini.

Apple's software suite includes Safari, Messages, Photos, Maps, Notes, and Reminders. Its services, including News and Apple TV, also have applications. Pages, Keynote, and Numbers are still in the dock by default, but they're the Creator Studio subscription versions, which require a subscription to get full features, including AI functionality, royalty-free graphics, and additional templates.

Apple sells the MacBook Pro with PROLED XDR Display with a one-year warranty. AppleCare+ extends protection for $17.99 per month or $179.99 per year, and also adds accidental damage and drop coverage.

We reviewed the MacBook Pro with PROLED XDR Display in a high-end, $7,399 configuration with an M5 Max (18 CPU cores, 40 GPU cores), 128GB of memory, and 2TB of storage.

The cheapest versions of these machines are a $2,899 14-inch system with a cut-down M5 Pro featuring 15 CPU cores and 16 GPU Cores or a $3,399 16-inch laptop with the M5 Pro featuring an 18-core CPU and 20-core GPU. Both base configurations come with 24GB of RAM and 1TB of storage, as well as the glossy screen option. (Adding the matte "Nano-texture" glass adds $150 and also nets you a polishing cloth, which isn't included with the glossy screen.)

Moving to the M5 Max moves you to $4,499 on the 14-inch laptop and $4,799 on the 16-incher for an 18-core CPU and a cut-down 32-core GPU. The full 40-core GPU is another $300.

RAM starts at 24GB, and costs $600 to goto 48GB or, on the 16-incher, $1,000 for 64GB. There's also 36GB and 128GB RAM options that also require 2TB of storage and an M5 Max (like our review unit). Storage prices are also astronomical, with a $1,000 jump between the 2TB and 4TB tier. (If you want 16TB, which I think will be a relatively rare choice, it's a $7,000 bump that requires the high-end M5 Max).

These are exceptionally expensive computers. Apple's 14- and 16-inch MacBook Pros without the OLED screens are still there, however, starting at $1,999 for a 14-inch notebook with M6.

Apple sells the systems at full price, or divided up into 12 monthly payments through an installment plan. They're also available for lease through Apple Upgrade.

The MacBook Pro with PROLED XDR display isn't a radical redesign, but it has a bunch of small changes that add up to significant improvements over previous models. The tandem OLED screen is a treat to look at, and the weight reduction is significant for anyone carrying their laptop around on the go. Even the deeper bass from the speakers is a real treat.

Performance from the M5 Max is still strong, though if you're doing simple work, you can get much better single-core performance out of an M6 Macbook Pro (though you'll give up everything else).

With no major redesign, however, Apple hasn't done anything to make touching the laptop any more comfortable than its competitors. It ultimately does get tiring. That being said, the few gestures and adjustments Apple has made to macOS are helpful, if not revolutionary. Touch really is here because it's table stakes at this point. Some will use it and others won't. It's not the selling point, other than that it's a debut for the feature.

Far more Mac user deserves a screen this good, and I hope that some flavor of OLED, even if it's not tandem OLED, eventually makes it to the entry-level MacBook Pro and even the MacBook Air. Touch, too, could be popular with younger people who grew up with all touch screens and go for the Air or the MacBook Neo.

But the starting price for the systems with PROLED displays are high, and upgrades make them exceptionally expensive. For now, the best screens in the Mac lineup (and arguably the industry) are limited to just these systems. We've waited a long time for OLED on the Mac, and for many, they'll still be waiting until it gets more affordable.

Those with the deep pockets to make the investment, though, are in for one heck of a screen.

Moto Watch Fit 27 launches sometime in the future in three colorways: Pantone Stratified Sea, Pantone Pigeon, and Pantone Ballerina.

#10205. How to Use CSS Attribute Selectors to Build More Flexible User Interfaces

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

When developing a website, developers often rely on CSS classes to style buttons, forms, navigation elements, and other interface components. This approach works well, but some situations require styles to depend on an element's HTML attributes rather than additional class names.

CSS attribute selectors provide a practical solution. They allow developers to target elements based on whether an attribute exists, what value it contains, or whether its value matches a particular pattern. Used carefully, they can simplify styling and make reusable components easier to maintain.

An attribute selector targets an HTML element using square brackets. For example, a developer can select every input field that has a required attribute:

This rule applies to inputs with the required attribute, regardless of their input type.

Developers can also target a specific attribute value:

This is useful when different input types need different visual treatments without introducing additional classes into the HTML.

Forms are a practical place to apply attribute selectors because HTML already provides attributes that describe how individual fields behave.

Consider a registration form containing email, password, and telephone fields. Each input can be styled according to its purpose:

The CSS can target these fields independently:

This approach keeps the markup descriptive while allowing the stylesheet to respond to the attributes already present in the document.

One important distinction is that required indicates a validation requirement, while :invalid represents an input's current validity state. These should not be treated as interchangeable.

CSS provides several matching operators that help developers target more specific attribute values.

These operators can be useful when working with links, downloadable files, language attributes, and reusable components.

For example, a website might want to distinguish PDF downloads from ordinary navigation links:

This adds a small visual indicator to links whose URLs end in .pdf.

Attribute selectors can also help identify links with different destinations or behaviors.

For example, external links that use HTTPS can receive a subtle visual indicator:

However, matching an HTTPS URL does not necessarily prove that a link points to an external website. Internal links can also use HTTPS, so developers should consider the actual navigation requirements before applying special styling.

For more examples of selector combinations and matching rules, SitePoint's guide to CSS Attribute Selectors provides a useful reference.

Although attribute selectors are flexible, they should not replace every class-based styling decision.

A selector such as [class*="button"] can match unintended elements if their class names happen to contain the same text. Similarly, broad attribute rules may affect components that were not originally intended to receive those styles.

For predictable results, use exact attribute matching whenever possible and prefer descriptive classes for major component styling.

Developers should also remember that CSS selectors only control presentation. They do not replace JavaScript validation, server-side security checks, or proper HTML semantics.

The MDN documentation on attribute selectors provides further details about selector syntax, matching behavior, and supported operators.

Before adding attribute selectors to a large stylesheet, inspect the affected elements using browser developer tools.

Check whether the selector matches the intended elements.

Test fields with different attribute values.

Verify disabled, required, and invalid form states.

Check navigation links and downloadable files.

Test the layout on mobile and desktop screens.

Review whether existing component styles are being unintentionally overridden.

This process helps identify unexpected matches before they affect the production interface.

CSS attribute selectors give developers another way to create flexible and maintainable interfaces without adding unnecessary classes to every HTML element.

They are particularly useful for form styling, link identification, and components that already contain meaningful HTML attributes. By choosing precise selectors, understanding matching operators, and testing changes before deployment, developers can use this feature to write cleaner CSS while keeping their markup descriptive and organized.

#10202. Accelerating Qwen3-8B Agent on Intel® Core™ Ultra with Depth-Pruned Draft Models

#10201. Announcing New vLLM and SGLang Inference Engines on TPU

As part of our broader announcements for PyTorch today, we are excited to launch native PyTorch backends for vLLM (developed in partnership with Inferact) and SGLang (developed in partnership with RadixArk).

For years, TPUs have powered large-scale AI workloads with high performance and efficiency. With the rise of agentic and multimodal workloads, serving efficiently is more important than ever. Both engines already run on TPU through JAX-based backends, but we kept hearing from developers that they wanted to customize and debug their serving stack in PyTorch.

So we started in earnest on TorchTPU, which runs PyTorch natively on TPU. We then built new vLLM and SGLang engines on top of it.

Our goal is for the PyTorch community to bring models to TPU and make them fast using the tools developers are already familiar with. The engines come with optimized, production-ready serving features and custom Pallas kernels.

Serving at scale requires co-design across every layer of the technology stack, from silicon to compilers to orchestration. Today's launch of new inference engines is at the central part of an integrated, open TPU ecosystem.

TorchTPU makes the TPU a native PyTorch device. Tensors are managed as standard PyTorch tensors on a tpu device with no wrapper types, code runs eagerly or compiles via torch.compile using XLA, and collectives go through torch.distributed.

Because each device runs its own PyTorch process, both engines keep each framework’s worker model, scheduler, KV cache manager, API server and so forth, with TPU implementations underneath.

Most models use the standard upstream PyTorch model definitions in both engines, with TPU-specific optimizations in kernels and layer implementations. Custom kernels written in Pallas are called directly from PyTorch as custom operators inside the compiled graph. This platform-plugin design reuses as much of each framework as possible while letting TPU optimizations move at their own pace.

Both engines are built for the full range of production workloads.

Agentic workloads in particular demand several of these features simultaneously. Because agents repeatedly resend expanding multi-turn conversations, serving them efficiently requires finding the cached prefix instead of recomputing it. Both engines keep reusable prefixes in HBM and, with host offload turned on, automatically move older prefixes to host memory as a second tier.

Two of these features move the KV cache away from the device that computed it. Disaggregated serving runs prefill and decode on separate TPU hosts, so each request's KV cache has to travel from the prefill host to the decode host.Tiered KV offload moves the part of the KV cache that no longer fits in HBM to the host's DRAM and secondary storage with Google Cloud Managed Lustre, and brings it back when a later request reuses the prefix. Either way, the transfer has to overlap computation or it adds directly to latency.

Both engines use TPU Sync for this. It is an open-source C++ library for KV cache transfer and management on TPU. It reads the device buffers behind PyTorch tensors directly, so a transfer needs no extra staging copy on the host, and it runs transfers on its own threads outside Python while prefill and decode continue. vLLM-TorchTPU exposes it through vLLM's KV connector interface. SGLang-TorchTPU uses it as a disaggregation transfer backend and as the I/O backend for HiCache.

Both engines are already running at scale in production with preview customers. To provide transparent, reproducible proof of our performance, we benchmark in public through the community-standard SemiAnalysis InferenceX project.

Building on our initial submission of Qwen 3.5 on Ironwood TPU (TPU 7x), our engineering teams have further optimized the stack, bringing the lowest cost per million tokens overall to an industry leading 2.01 cents. Alongside these improvements, we have also published new state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance results to AgentX, the project's dedicated track for evaluating agentic long-context and multi-turn workloads. Ironwood TPU leads across most of the Pareto frontier. We look forward to continuously updating our public performance profiles as we bring more models and configurations to the leaderboard.

We did not build these backends in a vacuum. By embracing PyTorch and working directly with open-source project maintainers, we ensure these engines remain upstream-aligned and community-first:

This partnership is bringing SGLang's stellar team to build for TPU, delivering an engine that is already running in production at Meta and battle-tested at scale across demanding real-world scenarios.

Together, we integrated a high-performance Pallas megakernel for the 2.8T-parameter Kimi K3 model that runs the entire decode step as one kernel, serving more than 700 tokens per second to a single user on Ironwood TPU. Building on this work, Inferact recently led our first preliminary AgentX submission for Kimi K3 on the vLLM-TorchTPU stack. This initial milestone already demonstrates exceptional performance per TCO in ultra-high-interactivity workloads, and the rapid increase in the maturity of the vLLM and PyTorch ecosystem on TPU for doing new frontier OSS models bring-up, with follow-up submissions set to unlock even greater efficiency through larger scale prefill/decode disaggregation.

To foster continuous open-source contribution, vLLM is also launching a first-of-its-kind community contribution TPU capacity pool to provide free TPU compute to developers who actively contribute to the vLLM engine.

As the models get larger, we are evolving both engines to make large-scale TPU inference deployments performant and efficient. The major features for production-grade agentic workloads are already supported. More performance optimizations and advanced features are under way – open a Github issue to request collaboration on yours!

Across the two engines, most of the popular open model families are supported, including Qwen, DeepSeek, GLM, Kimi, GPT-OSS, Gemma and more. The team is investing in making more models available to run natively on TPUs, including Day 0 support for new open models. We also want to give the community the tools and TPU capacity to tune performance for their own workloads and configurations.

vLLM-JAX and SGLang-JAX remain important projects. Both will continue to be maintained, supported, and available for the community to use and adapt.

#10195. How to Get a YouTube API Key and Use the Data API (2026)

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

You need a YouTube API key the moment your app has to read anything from YouTube: a video's length, its view count, a channel's latest uploads. Getting one takes about five minutes. Using it well takes a little longer, because YouTube changed how its quota works in June 2026, and plenty of guides still describe the old rules.

In this guide we'll get a key, lock it down and test it. Then we'll use it from Node.js for three real jobs: adding up the length of a playlist, listing a channel's videos without spending a search, and estimating what a video earned. Along the way we'll cover what the YouTube API costs (nothing), how the 2026 quota works, and the errors you're most likely to hit.

A YouTube API key identifies your project to the YouTube Data API v3. With just a key, you can read public data:

A key isn't enough for anything that touches someone's account. Uploading videos, editing playlists or reading private data needs OAuth 2.0, where the user signs in and grants your app access.

You'll need a Google account. The YouTube Data API is free, so there's nothing to pay for.

The restrictions matter more than they look. A key is just a string in a URL, and anyone who copies an unrestricted one can spend your daily quota for you. With an API restriction, the key is useless for other Google APIs. With an application restriction, it only works from your server or your site. Changes to restrictions can take up to five minutes to apply, so don't panic if a request fails straight after you save.

Before writing any code, check the key with a single request. This one asks for the first video ever uploaded to YouTube, and uses the fields parameter to trim the response down to four values:

That request cost 1 unit of your daily quota, which brings us to the question everyone asks.

Yes. There's no charge per request and no paid plan. Instead you get a daily quota, and since June 1, 2026 it's split into separate buckets:

Google's quota calculator lists the cost of every method. Quotas reset at midnight Pacific Time. Every request costs at least 1 unit, including requests that fail, so a retry loop around a broken call can use up a day's quota on its own.

If you've read older guides, search is the big change. A search used to cost 100 units out of the same 10,000, so 100 searches emptied your whole day. Uploads were worse still, at about 1,600 units each until December 2025. Now both have their own buckets, and the 10,000 units are left for everything else.

To give you a feel for what 10,000 units buys, here's what each example in this guide costs:

You can't pay for more. To go above the defaults, fill in the YouTube API Services Audit and Quota Extension Form, which is linked from Google's page on quota and compliance audits. YouTube first audits your project to check it follows the YouTube API Services Terms of Service, then someone from the API team gets back to you. Google doesn't give a timeline, so apply well before you need the extra quota.

Most projects never need to. The rest of this guide is about getting a lot done with very few units.

The examples need Node.js 20.6 or later, for the built-in fetch and the --env-file flag. There are no packages to install. Make a folder with a .env file in it:

Every example imports one small helper. It builds the URL, adds your key and turns API errors into readable messages:

Run any example with node --env-file=.env followed by the file name.

YouTube doesn't tell you how long a playlist is. To work it out, you need two methods:

Durations come back in ISO 8601 format. PT15M33S is 15 minutes 33 seconds, PT1H2M3S adds an hour, and a stream longer than a day looks like P1DT2H3M4S. The toSeconds() function below handles all three.

Here it is on freeCodeCamp's Full Courses in One Video playlist:

That's 184 hours of courses for 4 units of quota. Private and deleted videos still show up in a playlist's items, but videos.list doesn't return them, which is why the script prints "52 of 52 videos available". On a playlist with removed videos, the two numbers will differ.

This is the core of the free YouTube playlist length calculator I built on createthat.ai. The live version adds a target time box that works out the speed you need to finish by a deadline, and it accepts channel, video and Shorts links as well as playlists.

The obvious way to find a channel's videos is to search for them. Don't: search is now capped at 100 calls a day, and it isn't built to list everything a channel has posted. Every channel has an uploads playlist instead, and you can reach it in two cheap calls:

Two units, and none of your 100 daily searches. To get every upload rather than the newest five, page through the playlist with the same pageToken loop as Example 1.

videos.list with part=statistics returns a video's view, like and comment counts. Dislike counts have been private since December 2021 and favoriteCount is always 0, so don't build anything on those. The script also copes with a missing like or comment count, which you'll see on some videos.

This time the fields parameter trims the response to the values we use. That makes the response smaller, but it doesn't change the cost: the call is 1 unit either way.

Here it is on freeCodeCamp's Learn Python full course, one of the most-watched videos in that playlist:

Treat the last line as a rough range, not an answer. The Data API doesn't share anyone's revenue, so any estimate is views multiplied by an RPM: what a creator keeps per 1,000 views. RPM moves a lot with the topic and where the viewers live, and the estimate assumes the video shows ads, which not every channel does.

And sometimes you don't need an API at all. When I built a font tester, I needed a list of Google Fonts. The Google Fonts Developer API would have meant a key and a call on every visit, for a list that barely changes. Instead I generated a file of the 500 most popular fonts once, from Google's public font metadata, and the page loads it like any other file. No key, no quota and nothing to fail.

When a call fails, the response's error.message and error.details[0].reason tell you why. These are the ones you'll see most:

For the invalid key error, the full response looks like this:

Yes. Creating a key and calling the YouTube Data API costs nothing. You're limited by the daily quota instead, and there's no paid tier: more quota comes through an audit.

In the Google Cloud console, select your project and go to APIs & Services > Credentials. Your keys are listed under API keys, with a Show key option next to each one.

It works, but anyone can read the key from your page. If you do it, restrict the key to your website and to the YouTube Data API v3. A small server route that calls the API for you is safer, and it gives you somewhere to cache results.

By default, 100 searches, 100 uploads and 10,000 units for everything else. Most reads cost 1 unit and videos.list takes 50 IDs per call, so that's enough for the details of up to 500,000 videos a day.

Getting a YouTube API key is the easy part: one project, one API to enable, one key and two restrictions. The 2026 quota is generous if you work with it:

All three examples together cost 7 units, out of 10,000. From here, try commentThreads.list to pull a video's comments 100 at a time, or extend Example 2 to page through a channel's entire upload history and add it all up with the code from Example 1.

#10185. Les supermarchés de l'UE paient d'un firmware de réfrigérateur non mis à jour

Breaking cybersecurity news, news analysis, commentary, and other content from around the world, with an initial focus on the Middle East & Africa, the Asia Pacific, Europe, and Latin America.

Des centaines de magasins ont subi des pertes se chiffrant en millions d'euros parce qu'ils avaient fait appel à des tiers pour gérer le micrologiciel OT obsolète qui pilotait leurs systèmes de climatisation.

This translation is provided by our internal AI translation tool.

Des centaines de supermarchés français ont subi cette semaine des pertes s'élevant à plusieurs dizaines, voire plusieurs centaines de millions d'euros, à la suite de cyberattaques qui ont perturbé leurs systèmes de réfrigération.

Dans la nuit de lundi, des pirates informatiques ont piraté les contrôleurs des systèmes de réfrigération commerciaux de grandes chaînes de supermarchés françaises. Ils ont transformé les réfrigérateurs, les congélateurs et les chambres froides en appareils de chauffage, faisant grimper leur température jusqu’à 50 degrés Celsius (plus de 120 degrés Fahrenheit), ce qui a complètement abîmé tous les aliments qu’ils contenaient et aurait même provoqué quelques petits incendies. L’identité et les motivations de l’auteur de cette attaque restent inconnues.

Les contrôleurs de réfrigération permettent aux commerçants de régler et de surveiller à distance la température de leurs réfrigérateurs, 24 heures sur 24 et 7 jours sur 7. Il s’ensuit donc que les gérants de magasin auraient pu être alertés ou se rendre compte manuellement que quelque chose n’allait pas. Pour empêcher cela, des sources françaises indiquent que les attaquants ont prolongé les cycles de dégivrage des systèmes ciblés — processus au cours duquel les congélateurs font fondre intentionnellement le givre et la glace accumulés — afin que le réchauffement passe pour un entretien de routine.

Related:EU Grocers Pay a Steep Price for Unpatched Fridge Firmware

Selon les propres rapports des entreprises, les attaques ont touché un peu plus de 700 magasins d’alimentation et commerces de détail, tous au cours de la même nuit. Un analyste de la société française de cybersécurité Sekoia a déclaré à Reuters que l’Espagne, le Portugal, la Pologne, l’Allemagne et la Belgique avaient également été touchés, et qu’une grande chaîne de supermarchés aux Pays-Bas avait mis ses systèmes hors service par mesure de précaution, selon l’AP. Une seule chaîne française, Intermarché, a fait état de pertes s’élevant à plusieurs dizaines de millions d’euros.

Pire encore, les experts soulignent que ces attaques auraient très certainement pu être évitéesgrâce à des mesures élémentaires de cybersécurité.

Les premiers rapports suggéraient que les attaques de lundi étaient liées aux contrôleurs de réfrigération du fabricant danois Danfoss. Danfoss occupe une place importante dans le domaine des solutions de contrôle pour les supermarchés et la grande distribution alimentaire, avec plus de 50 000 installations de sa technologie à travers le monde, selon son site web.

Related:Taiwan Bullet Train Hack Highlights Cybersecurity Gaps in Rail Systems

Le 9 août, des chercheurs en cybersécurité de l’équipe Team82 de Claroty ont révélé trois vulnérabilités dans la plateforme AK-SM 800A de Danfoss. Ces failles — référencées sous les numéros CVE-2025-41450, CVE-2025-41451 et CVE-2025-41452 — permettaient aux attaquants de procéder à l’exécution de code à distance (RCE) ou à un déni de service (DoS) contre les parcs de réfrigération des entreprises. Danfoss a corrigé ces failles mais comptait sur ses clients pour mettre à jour eux-mêmes leurs systèmes.

Dans une déclaration à Reuters, Danfoss a rejeté la responsabilité sur les clients utilisant un micrologiciel obsolète, indiquant qu’elle « conseillait depuis longtemps à ses clients de maintenir les systèmes pris en charge à jour avec le dernier micrologiciel et de migrer les systèmes hérités vers des solutions de dernière génération ».Le fabricant d'équipement d'origine (OEM) a fourni davantage d'informations à une publication spécialisée dans la réfrigération, Cooling Post. Selon lui, la « majorité » des unités concernées fonctionnaient sous AK-SM 800 ou son prédécesseur, l'AK-SC355. Ces deux systèmes ont depuis longtemps atteint leur fin de vie : le premier en 2021, le second en 2016.

Related:AI-Driven Cyberattack on Mexico Couldn't Breach OT Systems

Mardi, le CERT national français (CERT-FR) avait identifié qu’une autre plateforme de contrôleurs de réfrigération — « XWEB », des fournisseurs Dixell et de sa société mère Copeland — avait également été impliquée dans les attaques. Il n’a pas précisé quelles vulnérabilités ou versions de micrologiciel pouvaient être en cause, bien que les indices circonstanciels indiquent désormais clairement que les auteurs de ces menaces s’appuient sur les recherches de Team82. Le jour même où ils ont révélé ces vulnérabilités affectant Danfoss, ces mêmes chercheurs ont divulgué 23 autres touchant XWEB Pro, dont 21 de gravité élevée.

Le 26 août, une série étrange de rapports similaires a commencé à filtrer depuis des installations militaires américaines.Les groupes frigorifiques tombaient en panne. Dans chaque base où cela se produisait, les gens ont probablement pensé qu’il s’agissait d’un problème électrique inhabituel. Mais peu à peu, de plus en plus de sites ont signalé le même phénomène.

Après être tombée sur ces rapports étranges sur les réseaux sociaux, une journaliste indépendante a émis une hypothèse hasardeuse. « Je suis suffisamment lucide pour savoir que cela semble insensé, mais je vous demande de me suivre », a écrit M. Elizabeth sur Substack. « Je pense qu’il y a une probabilité non nulle que quelqu’un ait piraté les congélateurs des commissariats. »

À ce jour, l’armée américaine n’a pas confirmé qui ou quoi était à l’origine des pannes survenues dans les cantines réparties sur au moins 14 bases de l’armée de l’air, installations de la marine et postes de l’armée de terre distincts. Il se pourrait que ce soit une coïncidence que ces attaques aient eu lieu seulement deux semaines après la divulgation des vulnérabilités par Danfoss.

Ce serait d’autant plus une coïncidence que la Defense Commissary Agency (DeCA) — l’agence centrale du ministère de la Défense (DoD) chargée de superviser les magasins militaires — a explicitement mentionné avoir utilisé les technologies de Danfoss par le passé. Par exemple, dans un modèle de cahier des charges techniquedestiné aux prestataires des magasins militaires datant de juin 2022, la DeCA citait le Danfoss AK-SM 800A comme « base de conception » pour les différentes unités de contrôle.

Fort de son expérience, Bill Moore, PDG de Xona Systems, se souvient : « Dans bon nombre de ces magasins physiques — mais aussi dans les usines de fabrication, les centrales électriques, les sites pétroliers et gaziers, c’est le cas partout —, le plus gros problème est que les organisations parviennent plutôt bien à segmenter leurs propres réseaux. Mais en matière de technologies opérationnelles (OT), il y a tous ces équipementiers et tiers qui ont besoin d’un accès. Et les entreprises n’y ont pas vraiment prêté attention. »

En accordant à de nombreux fournisseurs et équipementiers l’accès à leurs systèmes, les entreprises finissent par s’exposer à des attaques à distance. « Et il n’y a littéralement aucune visibilité ni gouvernance sur bon nombre de ces connexions. Je dirais que c’est vrai dans un pourcentage élevé de cas — la moitié, peut-être plus, dans l’ensemble. Elles laissent en quelque sorte tous leurs équipementiers agir à leur guise, mais nous avons constaté que de nombreux pirates compromettent ces connexions tierces », déplore-t-il.

Dans un e-mail adressé à CNBC, Les Mousquetaires — société mère d’Intermarché et de Netto — a expliqué que l’attaque avait eu lieu via un prestataire de services chargé des systèmes de réfrigération dans certains de ses magasins.

« En résumé : déconnectez vos équipements d’Internet », déclare Andrew Ginter, vice-président chargé de la sécurité industrielle chez Waterfall Security Solutions. Les recherches effectuées cette semaine sur Shodan ont par exemple révélé des milliers de systèmes Danfoss exposés sur Internet, dont environ 1 500 rien qu’en France.

« Dans un monde idéal, les entreprises protégeraient leurs systèmes de contrôle derrière un accès à distance contrôlé par du matériel », explique M. Ginter, « mais même un simple pare-feu entre ces appareils et Internet serait déjà utile. »

Pour les pare-feu de base, il conseille aux entreprises :

De mettre en place une règle « tout refuser »

De n’autoriser les connexions qu’à partir d’adresses IP spécifiques

D’appliquer un mot de passe et une authentification à deux facteurs à ces connexion

De mettre à jour automatiquement le pare-feu, pour éviter de l’oublier

Utilisent des appareils de qualité commerciale ou industrielle, et non grand public ; de nombreux appareils grand public ne proposent pas de mises à jour, ou ne se mettent pas à jour rapidement

Enfin, ajoute-t-il, « Remplacez le pare-feu strictement tous les quatre ans. Pourquoi ? Parce que si nous avons des appareils exposés à Internet, neuf fois sur dix, nous n’avons pas d’équipe de sécurité, et nous ne remarquerons pas quand nos pare-feu ne seront plus pris en charge et que les mises à jour automatiques cesseront. »

« Les mesures de base ne suffisent pas pour les infrastructures critiques de grande envergure ou l’industrie lourde, mais elles contribueront grandement à empêcher les hacktivistes de s’introduire dans vos congélateurs », conclut Ginter. « Retirez vos équipements d’Internet. Faites-le de toute urgence. »

#10182. Top 6 AI Image Generators in 2026: Features, Strengths, and Limits

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A designer who once spent an afternoon searching stock libraries can now describe the picture they want and have four versions in under a minute. AI image generators have moved from curiosity to everyday tool for blog headers, product mockups, ad concepts, game art and slide decks. The quality gap between tools has narrowed, but the differences that remain matter a lot: how well each follows a detailed prompt, whether it can render readable text, how it handles edits, and what you are allowed to do with the result.

This guide compares six of the best-known options, what each does well and where each falls short. Models, plans and limits change often, so check each vendor's own pages before you commit to a workflow.

OpenArt’s AI image generator puts several image models behind one interface. Its generator page lists models including GPT Image 2.5, Grok Imagine, Qwen Image 3, Nano Banana 2 and Seedream 5.0 Pro, and lets you create images from text prompts or reference photos. It also lists tools for camera angle control, upscaling, collages and a Character Builder for keeping characters consistent across scenes, with 4K export.

Best for: Creators who want to compare several models on the same prompt without juggling separate subscriptions and accounts.

Limitations: The page offers a free start but does not state credit amounts or plan details, and it does not address commercial use, so check the pricing and licensing pages before using results for client work. Output quality depends on which model you pick.

OpenAI's image generation in ChatGPT works inside the chat interface, which makes iteration natural: generate an image, then ask for changes in plain language. OpenAI highlights accurate text rendering, the ability to follow prompts with many distinct objects, and consistent characters across multi-turn edits. You can also upload reference images and have the model transform them. It handles both photorealistic and stylized looks.

Best for: Marketers and writers who want to describe, refine and re-describe an image without learning a new tool.

Limitations: OpenAI notes that tall images such as posters can be cropped too tightly, and that dense small text, multilingual text and precise graphs can still go wrong. Images can take up to a minute to render. All generated images carry C2PA metadata identifying their source.

Gemini's image generation runs on Google's Nano Banana models. Google highlights character consistency, text rendering in many languages, combining several photos, precise editing and 2K resolution. A free tier covers image generation and editing, while the most advanced model requires a Google AI plan. You start from the "Create images" option in the Gemini app, then prompt or upload a picture to edit.

Best for: People who need to keep the same subject across several images, or who want to edit existing photos quickly.

Limitations: Usage limits apply, and the strongest model is behind a paid plan. Images include an invisible SynthID watermark and a visible AI label, which is good for transparency but may not suit every use.

Midjourney is an independent research lab known for images with a strong, painterly look. Its official site points users to a sign-up flow and to a Discord community and help center for support. Many creators choose it for concept art, mood boards and illustration where style matters more than literal accuracy.

Best for: Artists, art directors and game designers who want rich, stylized results and enjoy exploring variations.

Limitations: I could not confirm current pricing, trial terms or feature details from Midjourney's public pages, so check them yourself before subscribing. The workflow can feel less like a normal app than the others on this list, and it is not the easiest route for precise, text-heavy graphics.

Adobe Firefly is built for professionals who already work in Creative Cloud. Adobe states that its Firefly models are commercially safe, trained on licensed Adobe Stock and public domain content, and outputs carry Content Credentials that show AI involvement. It offers text-to-image, image-to-image, sketch-to-image and style transfer, plus editing tools such as background removal, object removal, upscaling and generative expand. It connects with Photoshop, Illustrator, Lightroom and Adobe Express, and also lets you use partner models from other companies.

Best for: Designers and brand teams who need clear licensing and want AI inside the apps they already use.

Limitations: Plans work on a credit system, so heavy use adds up. Adobe lists a free tier with limited daily generations and paid plans starting at about $9.99 a month, but confirm current terms on its site.

Ideogram built its reputation on a problem that trips up many generators: readable text. Its current models are presented as producing clean typography in different layouts, with attention to prompt fidelity and reliable editing. It also offers an API with access to a catalog of models, and has released open weights for one earlier model for local use and customization under its license.

Best for: Posters, social graphics, logo concepts and anything where words are part of the image.

Limitations: The pages I checked did not describe a free plan, so look at current plans before relying on it. Like every generator, it still needs a human to proofread the text it produces.

Start with the job. If you want to compare several models side by side, try OpenArt. If you want to talk your way to a finished image, try ChatGPT or Gemini. If you need images that will sit inside a branded design workflow, Firefly is the safer fit. If you are chasing a distinctive look, Midjourney deserves a trial. If text must be perfect, test Ideogram.

Run the same prompt in two or three tools before paying. Differences in composition, hands, faces and text show up quickly, and they matter more than any feature list.

Reviews only go so far, because your own use case is what counts. Pick one realistic job, for example a 16:9 header image for a blog post about web performance, and run the same prompt through two or three tools. Write the prompt once and paste it unchanged, so the comparison is fair:

Then score each result on five questions: Did it follow every instruction? Are there odd details in hands, edges or objects? Is any text spelled correctly? Does it still look good when cropped to a square? Could you license it for your project? Ten minutes of this teaches more than any feature list, and it shows how much editing each tool needs before you can publish.

Most readers of this guide will put these images on web pages, where a few habits matter more than the generator you choose.

There is no single best AI image generator. The right pick depends on whether you value conversation, style, licensing, control or typography. Test two or three on a real project, compare the output, and keep a human in charge of the final choice. Whatever you generate, review it for errors and label AI-made images clearly where your audience would expect it.

Are AI-generated images free to use commercially? It depends on the provider and your plan. Read the licensing terms for the tool you use, and consider vendors that publish how their models were trained if you work for clients.

Which AI image generator is best for text in images? Ideogram and ChatGPT both highlight text rendering, and Gemini lists it as a feature. Test each with your own wording and proofread the result.

Do AI images have watermarks? Some do. OpenAI adds C2PA metadata, Google adds an invisible SynthID watermark and a visible label, and Adobe attaches Content Credentials.

Can I use more than one image model without separate accounts? Yes. Platforms such as OpenArt put several models in one interface, which makes side-by-side testing easier. Check the licensing terms for each model you use.

Should I add alt text to AI-generated images? Yes, when the image carries meaning. Describe what it shows or why it is there. Purely decorative images can use an empty alt attribute, following the W3C guidance.

#10177. Bringing Visual Graph Debugging to Custom Edge Compilers with Model Explorer

Model Explorer goes beyond high-level model graphs. See how Arm plugs in custom compiler IRs, verifies NPU delegation side-by-side, and maps cycle-level profiling traces directly onto your execution graph.

Machine learning models run locally on a wide range of hardware, from low-power edge devices to gaming graphics and physical AI systems. Running models efficiently on these targets requires compilation steps such as quantization, graph lowering, operator fusion, and hardware delegation.

Arm used Google AI Edge Model Explorer to give developers visibility into these pipelines. With Model Explorer's hierarchical graph visualization and plugin architecture, Arm built several open-source adapters that make edge model transformations easier to inspect.

When deploying a model to edge hardware, a framework change or operator constraint can alter the exported execution graph. Engineers often need to answer three questions:

Answering these questions by reading raw flatbuffers or text logs takes time, especially for large language models (LLMs) and neural networks. Arm needed a visual tool to inspect Arm-specific intermediate representations and hardware profiling traces.

To address these deployment challenges, Arm developed custom adapters for Google AI Edge Model Explorer to support several model formats (i.e., TOSA, VGF, and PTE), establishing a comprehensive visual workflow for on-device optimization. Using these extensions, engineering teams can validate exported pipelines, verify accelerator delegation, and pinpoint cycle-level performance bottlenecks directly on the model graph:

Arm engineers used Model Explorer to validate a PyTorch-trained Neural Super Sampling (NSS) model exported through the ExecuTorch Arm backend as a VGF file for Vulkan ML Extensions. While inspecting the VGF graph, the team found unintended TRANSPOSE operators introduced by a change in the ExecuTorch Arm backend.

Model Explorer enables users to inspect individual nodes of the model graph, such as the rescale operator depicted below, which displays the operator's attributes and SPIR-V tensor connections. This functionality assisted the Arm team in identifying and resolving the regression without requiring manual analysis of raw log files.

To optimize vision models for accelerators like the Arm Ethos-U85 NPU, Arm engineers used Model Explorer's split-pane view to compare CPU and NPU-delegated graphs side-by-side for a Ultralytics YOLO model.

The split-pane view showed the structural difference: the CPU graph displays each individual layer, while the NPU graph groups the network into a single EthosUBackend block between quantize and dequantize nodes. For partially supported models in LiteRT or ExecuTorch, this view helps identify un-delegated operators and split subgraphs.

To find latency bottlenecks on the Ethos-U NPU, Arm ran their model on their Fixed Virtual Platform (FVP) simulator and used Model Explorer's node data plugin to overlay cycle-accurate execution traces onto the graph. Color-coded heatmaps showed that a single TRANSPOSE node was the main latency bottleneck.

You can use Google AI Edge Model Explorer along with Arm's open-source adapters today to inspect and optimize your own on-device models locally via PyPI or GitHub.

Special thanks to the contributors across the Arm and Google AI Edge teams for their work on these adapters and integrations:

Product manager: Charlie Stokes (ARM), Derek Bekebrede

Designers: Ami Kubota, Ioannis Politis (ARM), Kathleen Low, Philip Watson (ARM)

Senior Sponsors: Bala Gattu (ARM), Cormac Brick, Joe Alderson (ARM), Matthias Grundmann, Sharbani Roy (ARM)

#10165. Which MCM Has the Best Revshare? A Developer’s Guide to Evaluating Ad Partners

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

An ad partner’s revenue share looks easy to compare: keeping 90% sounds better than keeping 80%. But the percentage does not tell a developer what will happen when the integration reaches production. Extra JavaScript, unstable ad slots, opaque reporting, and difficult rollbacks can change the value of an apparently attractive deal.

For teams maintaining an advertising-funded website, choosing a Multiple Customer Management (MCM) partner is partly an engineering decision. The integration affects page templates, consent handling, third-party requests, account access, and monitoring. Those changes deserve the same scrutiny as a new analytics SDK or payment integration.

This guide explains how to compare revenue shares on a consistent basis, review integration requirements, and test an ad setup without sacrificing usability. The goal is a repeatable evaluation process, rather than a universal winner.

Google Ad Manager’s MCM system lets a parent publisher manage a child publisher’s account or delegated inventory. The commercial agreement defines the partner’s services and compensation. Before comparing offers, ask which revenue streams the quoted percentage covers and whether the reporting figure is before or after that fee.

Use three separate lines in your evaluation spreadsheet:

Revenue base: record the amount to which the partner’s percentage applies. Advertiser spending, exchange-reported revenue, and publisher earnings are different figures. Do not treat them as interchangeable.

Partner compensation: identify the percentage, fixed charges, and any optional services. Ask whether the same terms apply to every demand source and ad format.

Adjustments: distinguish estimated revenue from finalized earnings, including invalid-traffic deductions and other corrections. Document when adjustments appear and which party bears them.

A common mistake is applying the partner fee twice because a dashboard already displays net revenue. Label each exported metric before using it in a comparison.

The two delegation models have different implications for access, reporting, and payments. Google’s documentation is the starting point for checking the proposed setup.

Manage Account gives the parent edit access within the child’s account while the child retains access and permissions. Payment facilitation can allocate eligible earnings from the child’s primary Ad Exchange account, but it does not automatically cover every third-party revenue stream. Confirm the invitation’s payment percentage and the separate commercial agreement.

Manage Inventory lets the parent manage delegated inventory in its own account, without access to the child account. Reporting for that inventory sits in the parent account. Google pays the parent for the relevant inventory, and the parent pays the child according to their agreement. Ask how you will obtain reports and reconcile payments.

Before implementation, document who can change tags, ad units, consent settings, and demand configuration. Agree on access for debugging and exporting historical data. A developer should be able to trace a production request to its configuration owner.

Use this table as a request-for-information checklist. It does not rank providers or assert that any named service supports a particular implementation. Obtain current terms and technical documentation for the product you are considering.

Apply the same standards to every proposal. The following examples show what a developer should ask during evaluation; each question is relevant across providers.

MonetizeMore offers publisher monetization products, including PubGuru and Traffic Cop. When evaluating its proposal, connect the commercial discussion to concrete integration questions: which scripts will load, who owns the configuration, and how will reported earnings be reconciled?

Request a sample report showing the revenue base, partner fee, and deductions as separate fields. Check whether the proposed plan includes every service shown in the sales demonstration.

Ask for a written ownership map covering Ad Manager access, tag deployment, configuration changes, and incident response. Identify who can restore the previous setup if delivery fails.

During a trial, compare representative templates and devices. Record script requests and main-thread activity alongside net revenue, using the same measurement definitions for the existing setup.

Decision criterion: choose on measured results and documented responsibilities, rather than assuming that a quoted split predicts final earnings.

For any proposal involving a header-bidding wrapper, including one from Setupad, ask how the wrapper fits into your existing page lifecycle.

Review bidder configuration, auction timeouts, and when the ad server receives targeting information. A missing bid and an unfilled ad slot are different problems.

Check whether your team can inspect the active configuration and reproduce an issue on a test page. Agree on who approves changes to bidders or timeout values.

Measure the complete sequence: consent readiness, auction start, bid responses, ad request, and rendering. Avoid attributing every delay to the same component.

Decision criterion: the implementation should be observable enough to explain failures, with commercial terms verified separately.

When assessing Publisher Collective or another managed partner, establish how the proposed setup could be tested on a limited, agreed scope.

Ask whether rollout can be restricted to selected templates or an eligible traffic cohort. Do not assume partial testing is available until the partner confirms it.

Record device, geography, page type, and consent state so differences in traffic mix do not masquerade as a revenue improvement.

Keep a dated deployment log connecting each configuration version with the reporting period.

Decision criterion: a measured comparison should separate implementation changes from changes in audience or demand.

A proposal from AdPushup or another optimization provider may involve changes to ad placement. Review the actual proposed changes with both design and engineering.

Check spacing, responsive behavior, keyboard navigation, and whether sticky placements obscure content or controls.

Reserve space for expected ad dimensions and test what happens when no ad is returned.

Compare layout stability before and after the change.

Decision criterion: additional earnings should be assessed alongside usability and performance, with explicit acceptance criteria.

For an Ezoic proposal, as for any managed platform, start with the exact integration method offered to your site.

List required changes to scripts, plugins, caching, DNS, or delivery infrastructure, if any. Do not assume that every deployment uses the same method.

Test interactions with your consent manager, content security policy, caching rules, and existing analytics.

Document how to remove the integration and restore the prior configuration.

Decision criterion: the operational cost should be visible before the first production rollout.

When reviewing a Monumetric proposal, establish which implementation work belongs to your team and which belongs to the provider.

Ask who creates ad slots, updates templates, and validates responsive sizes.

Request a support escalation process with the diagnostic information required for an incident: URL, timestamp, device, consent state, and affected slot.

Confirm onboarding costs and payment terms in writing.

Decision criterion: routine changes and production failures should have a clear owner.

Chrome DevTools helps trace third-party requests in the Network panel and investigate long tasks in the Performance panel. Capture a baseline before deployment, then repeat the same navigation and interaction sequence after deployment. Use comparable device and network settings.

Google Publisher Console helps investigate Google Publisher Tag ad slots and delivery issues. For a Prebid-based setup, use Prebid’s debugging tools to inspect the auction path. These tools answer different questions: whether the auction behaved as expected and whether the ad server delivered the requested slot.

PageSpeed Insights provides performance diagnostics; distinguish its lab results from available field data. Add web-vitals to your own monitoring when you need measurements associated with a deployment or cohort. Track LCP, INP, and CLS alongside revenue instead of treating a single Lighthouse score as the complete user experience.

Reserve responsive space for ad slots before content arrives, and investigate shifts when empty slots collapse. SitePoint’s guide to web performance optimization explains related techniques for loading JavaScript, reducing layout work, and managing long tasks.

With the same traffic volume, revenue definition, and deduction rate, the break-even increase in pre-partner revenue is: (current publisher share ÷ new publisher share) − 1. Express shares consistently as fractions or percentages. The table below shows the required lift; it is not a forecast these thresholds only describe the arithmetic. Different ad density, audience mix, deduction rates, or reporting bases can invalidate the comparison. Normalize to the same denominator, such as revenue per thousand pageviews, and compare matched periods or properly assigned concurrent cohorts.

Assume two hypothetical setups each receive one million pageviews. Here, RPM means revenue per thousand pageviews before the partner fee. For this simplified example, deductions reduce that revenue before the publisher share is applied.

Partner A: $10 pre-partner RPM × 1,000 groups of pageviews × 0.90 after deductions × 0.90 publisher share = $8,100 net revenue.

Partner B: $12 pre-partner RPM × 1,000 groups of pageviews × 0.98 after deductions × 0.80 publisher share = $9,408 net revenue.

The difference is $1,308 for the period despite Partner B’s lower share. These are illustrative inputs, not provider performance claims. Faster payment improves cash-flow timing; it does not, by itself, increase the revenue earned.

Use Playwright with mocked responses or test creatives in a controlled environment to check slow loading, empty slots, blocked scripts, and navigation between routes. Verify that the page remains usable when the ad system fails. Automated tests should not click live advertisements or generate artificial monetized traffic.

Separate unit tests for configuration logic, integration tests for component boundaries, and browser tests for user-visible behavior. SitePoint’s explanation of unit, functional, and integration testing provides a useful foundation. Define rollback criteria before launch, keep the control configuration available, and review finalized revenue alongside performance data before expanding the deployment.

There is no universal answer from the percentage alone. Compare current written offers using the same revenue base, then evaluate finalized net earnings, performance, access, and implementation effort.

Not necessarily. Reports may differ in timezone, currency, transaction coverage, fee treatment, and finalization date. Reconcile those definitions before treating a discrepancy as an integration bug.

Ask how renewals, volume tiers, optional services, and rate changes are handled. Record the applicable terms with the configuration and reporting period used for your evaluation.

No. If deductions and the revenue base are otherwise comparable, an 80% share needs 12.5% more pre-partner revenue to match a 90% share. Measure whether that lift exists rather than assuming it.

Do not treat any protection claim as a guarantee. Ask how suspected invalid traffic is handled, what evidence is available, and how later adjustments are reflected in your reports and payments.

Choose the setup that produces verifiable net value while meeting the site’s performance and operational requirements. A good evaluation leaves your team with reproducible measurements, understandable reports, and a practical rollback plan—not just a favorable percentage.

Cybersecurity In-Depth: Feature articles on security strategy, latest trends, and people to know.

In this video, Dark Reading editors discuss some of the news they didn't get a chance to cover, from busting up North Korea's stateside infrastructure in "Operation Lonestar Justice" to Staples' gift card bonanza.

The US government is striking back against the ever-persistent threat of fake IT worker schemes run by the Democratic People's Republic of Korea (DPRK).

In this episode of "What We Missed," Dark Reading's Rob Wright and Alex Culafi discuss some of the recent news events and topics that didn't make it into the publication, starting with FBI raids across more than 40 Texas residences that were allegedly running "laptop farms."

The law-enforcement action, dubbed “Operation Lonestar Roundup,” seized 187 devices, charged 14 people, and arrested individuals accused of facilitating North Korean threat actors running fake IT worker schemes. The schemes disrupted by Operation Lonestar Roundup impacted more than 70 US companies, according to the FBI.

Fake IT worker schemes typically involve DPRK actors masquerading as IT professionals and developers, either using fake or stolen identities, to gain employment at US companies. The threat actors then use their positions to exfiltrate sensitive data, steal intellectual property, and gather intelligence.
